Large deviation analysis for classes of interacting Bosonic cycle counts Thanks: M.D. is supported by EPSRC as part of the MASDOC CDT at the University of Warwick, Grant No. EP/HO23364/1
Stefan Adams and Matthew Dickson

Abstract
. This paper studies probabilistic mean-field models for interacting bosons at a positive temperature in the thermodynamic limit with random particle density. In particular, we prove large deviation principles for empirical cycle counts in all our models, and as such generalise recent work in [ ACK11 ] where upper and lower large deviation bounds do not match. Namely, on the one hand we generalise to the so-called grand canonical ensemble , and on the other hand, we consider classes of interaction potentials depending on the cycle counts which are not restricted to be positive. Our large deviation results provide representation formulae for the thermodynamic limit of the pressure which in turn leads to proving Bose-Einstein-condensation (BEC) in all our models. A primary focus and novelty is the pressure representation via extended large deviation analysis for the so-called hard-sphere, or HYL-model (Huang-Yang-Luttinger) studied in [ Lew86 ] . This model has negative counter terms in the Hamiltonian and shows BEC depending on the coupling constants.



2000 Mathematics Subject Classification
Primary 60F10; 60J65; 82B10; 81S40

1.
Introduction and main results

In this paper, we study probabilistic mean-field models for interacting bosons at positive temperature in the thermodynamic limit with random particle density. See Section
2.3
for the physical background. Our aim is to prove large deviation principles for empirical cycle counts and to prove the so-called Bose-Einstein condensation (BEC) phase transition in terms of the behaviour of the minimisers (zeroes) of our rate functions. This study is a direct continuation of recent work in
[
ACK11
]
, namely, on the one hand we generalise to the so-called
grand canonical ensemble
, and on the other hand we consider classes of interaction potentials depending solely on the cycle counts which are not restricted to be positive. This approach allows us to prove BEC for our models as well as improving the analysis in
[
ACK11
]
in that we obtain matching upper and lower large deviation bounds. The main focus and novelty is to obtain configurational cycle weights and to obtain a detailed and extended large deviation analysis for the so-called hard-sphere or HYL-model (Huang-Yang-Luttinger) studied in
[
Lew86
]
. In Section
2.3
we provide discussion on how our results relate to the ones in
[
BLP88
]
and
[
BCMP05
]
.

1.1.
The model

The main object is
the following symmetrised sum of Brownian bridge expectations,

Z_{\Lambda}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\rm bc}})}}(\beta,\mu)=\sum_{N=0}^{\infty}\frac{{\rm e}^{\beta\mu N}}{N!}\sum_{\sigma\in\mathfrak{S}_{N}}\int_{\Lambda}{\rm d}x_{1}\cdots\int_{\Lambda}{\rm d}x_{N}\bigotimes_{i=1}^{N}\boldsymbol{\mu}_{x_{i},x_{\sigma(i)}}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\rm bc,\beta}})}}\Big[\exp\Big\{-\sum_{1\leq i<j\leq N}\int_{0}^{\beta}v(|B_{s}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({i}})}}-B_{s}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({j}})}}|)\,{\rm d}s\Big\}\Big].

(1.1)

Here
\boldsymbol{\mu}_{x,y}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\rm bc,\beta}})}}
is the canonical Brownian bridge measure with boundary condition
{\rm bc}\in\{\varnothing,{\rm per},{\rm Dir}\}
, time horizon
\beta>0
, initial point
x\in\Lambda
and terminal point
y\in\Lambda
. The sum is on permutations
\sigma\in\mathfrak{S}_{N}
of
1,\dots,N
and
\mu\in\mathbb{R}
is the
chemical potential
. The
interaction potential

v\colon\mathbb{R}\to[0,\infty]
is measurable, decays sufficiently fast at infinity and is possibly infinite close to the origin. We assume that
\Lambda
is a measurable subset of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
with finite volume. The boundary condition
{\rm bc}=\varnothing
refers to the standard Brownian bridge, whereas for
{\rm bc}={\rm Dir}
, the expectation is on those Brownian bridge paths which stay in
\Lambda
over the time horizon
[0,\beta]
. In the case of periodic boundary condition,
{\rm bc}={\rm per}
, we consider Brownian bridges on the torus
\Lambda=(\mathbb{R}/L\mathbb{Z})^{d}
with side length
L
.

Our main motivation to study the quantity
Z_{\Lambda}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\rm bc}})}}(\beta,\mu)
is the fact that it is related to all
N
-body Hamilton operators

{\mathcal{H}}_{N,\Lambda}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\rm bc}})}}=-\sum_{i=1}^{N}\Delta_{i}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\rm bc}})}}+\sum_{1\leq i<j\leq N}v(|x_{i}-x_{j}|),\qquad x_{1},\dots,x_{N}\in\Lambda,\qquad\rm{bc}\in\{{\rm Dir},{\rm per}\},N\in\mathbb{N},

(1.2)

and
N
-particle Hilbert spaces
L_{2}(\Lambda)^{\otimes N}
,
where
\Delta_{i}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\rm bc}})}}
stands for the Laplacian with bc boundary condition.
More precisely,
Z_{\Lambda}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\rm bc}})}}(\beta,\mu)
is equal to the trace of the projection of the direct sum of the operators
\exp{\{-\beta{\mathcal{H}}_{N,\Lambda}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\rm bc}})}}\}}
to the set of all symmetric (i.e., permutation invariant) functions
(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{N}\to\mathbb{R},N\in\mathbb{N}
. This statement is proven via the Feynman-Kac formula, see
[
G70
]
or
[
BR97
]
. Hence, we call
Z_{\Lambda}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\rm bc}})}}(\beta,\mu)
a partition function. The cycle expansion of the partition function in Section
1.2
for vanishing interaction provides the cycle weights for our reference Poisson point process.

It is the main purpose of this paper to derive variational expressions for the
limiting pressure

p^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\rm bc}})}}(\beta,\mu)=\frac{1}{\beta}\lim_{\Lambda\uparrow\mathbb{R}^{d}}\frac{1}{|\Lambda|}\log Z_{\Lambda}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\rm bc}})}}(\beta,\mu),

(1.3)

where
\beta\in(0,\infty),\mu\in\mathbb{R}
,
d\in\mathbb{N}
and
{\rm bc}\in\{\varnothing,{\rm per},{\rm Dir}\}
for classes of interacting models. The existence of the thermodynamic limit in (
1.3
) with
{\rm bc}\in\{{\rm per},{\rm Dir}\}
under suitable assumptions on the interaction potential
v
can be shown by standard methods, see for example
[
Rue69
, Th. 3.58]
and
[
BR97
]
. We achieve this via large deviation principles for empirical cycle counts under mean-field cycle weights in Section
1.3
. Our large deviation results generalise previous results in
[
BLP88
]
for the HYL model in the way that our empirical cycle counts provide deeper information (higher level of large deviation analysis) and that we can study all possible interaction parameter choices. The analysis of the minimiser (zeroes) of our rate functions in Section
2.1
enables us to prove BEC in our various models using the particle density of the minimiser and the derivative of the pressure.

Our approach and the remainder of Section
1
can be summarized as follows. Since any permutation decomposes into cycles, and using the Markov property, the family of the
N
bridges in (
1.1
) decomposes into cycles of various lengths, i.e. into bridges that start and end at the same site, which is uniformly distributed over
\Lambda
. We conceive these initial-terminal sites as the points of a standard Poisson point process on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
and the cycles as marks attached to these points; see Section
1.2
for the relevant notation. In Proposition
1.1
below, we rewrite
Z_{\Lambda}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\rm bc}})}}(\beta,\mu)
in terms of an expectation over a reference process: the marked Poisson point process
\omega_{\rm P}
.

In Section
1.3
, we present our results on the large deviation principles for empirical cycle counts. Along the way, we also obtain representations of the limiting pressure in (
1.3
) for our models. The proofs for our large deviation principles are in Section
3
. In Section
2.1
we analyse all our rate functions and obtain expressions for the zeroes of the rate functions. This analysis leads to pressure representation formulae and to our results on BEC in Section
2.2
. All proofs for this analysis are in Section
4.1
and Section
4.2
, respectively. The physical interpretation, motivation and relevance are discussed in Section
2.3
. Definitions and details for the different boundary conditions are given in Appendix
A
, and in Appendix
B
we summarise definition and main properties of the Bose functions, and in Appendix
C
we give definitions and main properties of the Lambert function.

1.2.
Representation of the partition function

In this section, we introduce our representation of the partition function
Z_{\Lambda}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\rm bc}})}}(\beta,\mu)
for each boundary condition
{\rm bc}\in\{\varnothing,{\rm per},{\rm Dir}\}
in terms of an expectation over a marked Poisson point process. The main result of this section is Proposition
1.1
. We have to introduce some notation.

We begin with the mark space. The space of marks is defined as

E^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\rm bc}})}}=\bigcup_{k\in\mathbb{N}}{\mathcal{C}}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\rm bc}})}}_{k,\Lambda},\qquad{\rm bc}\in\{\varnothing,{\rm per},{\rm Dir}\},

(1.4)

where, for
k\in\mathbb{N}
, we denote by
{\mathcal{C}}_{k}={\mathcal{C}}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\varnothing}})}}_{k,\Lambda}
the set of continuous functions
f\colon[0,k\beta]\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
satisfying
f(0)=f(k\beta)
, equipped with the topology of uniform convergence. Moreover,

{\mathcal{C}}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\rm Dir}})}}_{k,\Lambda}
, (respectively
{\mathcal{C}}_{k,\Lambda}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\rm per}})}}
) is the space of continuous functions in
\Lambda
(respectively on the torus
\Lambda=(\mathbb{R}/\penalty L\mathbb{Z})^{d}
) with time horizon
[0,k\beta]
. We sometimes call the marks
cycles
. By
\ell\colon E^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\rm bc}})}}\to\mathbb{N}
we denote the canonical map defined by
\ell(f)=k
if
f\in{\mathcal{C}}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\rm bc}})}}_{k,\Lambda}
. We call
\ell(f)
the
length
of
f\in E
. When dealing with the empty boundary condition, we sometimes drop the superscript
\varnothing
.

We consider spatial configurations that consist of a locally finite set
\xi\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
of particles, and to each particle
x\in\xi
we attach a mark
f_{x}\in E^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\rm bc}})}}
satisfying
f_{x}(0)=x
. Hence, a configuration is described by the counting measure

\omega=\sum_{x\in\xi}\delta_{(x,f_{x})}

on
\mathbb{R}^{d}\times E
for the empty boundary condition (respectively on
\Lambda\times E^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\rm bc}})}}
for
{\rm bc}\in\{{\rm per},{\rm Dir}\}
).

We now introduce three marked Poisson point processes for the three boundary conditions. The one for the empty boundary condition will later serve as a reference process and is introduced separately first. The remaining two processes are defined in Appendix
A
.

Reference process.

Consider on
{\mathcal{C}}={\mathcal{C}}_{1}
the canonical Brownian bridge measure

\boldsymbol{\mu}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\varnothing,\beta}})}}_{x,y}(A)=\boldsymbol{\mu}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\beta}})}}_{x,y}(A)=\frac{\mathbb{P}_{x}(B\in A;B_{\beta}\in{\rm d}y)}{{\rm d}y},\qquad A\subset{\mathcal{C}}\mbox{ measurable}.

(1.5)

Here
B=(B_{t})_{t\in[0,\beta]}
is a Brownian motion in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
with generator
\Delta
, starting from
x
under
\mathbb{P}_{x}
. Then
\mu^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\beta}})}}_{x,y}
is a regular Borel measure on
{\mathcal{C}}
with total mass equal to the Gaussian density,

\boldsymbol{\mu}_{x,y}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\beta}})}}({\mathcal{C}})=g_{\beta}(x,y)=\frac{\mathbb{P}_{x}(B_{\beta}\in{\rm d}y)}{{\rm d}y}=(4\pi\beta)^{-d/2}{\rm e}^{-\frac{1}{4\beta}|x-y|^{2}}.

(1.6)

We write
\mathbb{P}_{x,y}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\beta}})}}=\boldsymbol{\mu}_{x,y}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\beta}})}}/g_{\beta}(x,y)
for the normalized Brownian bridge measure on
{\mathcal{C}}
.
Let

\omega_{\rm P}=\sum_{x\in\xi_{\rm P}}\delta_{(x,B_{x})},

be a Poisson point process on
\mathbb{R}^{d}\times E
with intensity measure equal to
\nu
, whose projection onto
\mathbb{R}^{d}\times{\mathcal{C}}_{k}
is equal to

\nu_{k}({\rm d}x,{\rm d}f)=\frac{1}{k}{\rm Leb}({\rm d}x)\otimes{\rm e}^{\beta\mu k}\mu_{x,x}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({k\beta}})}}({\rm d}f),\qquad k\in\mathbb{N}.

(1.7)

Alternatively, we can conceive
\omega_{\rm P}
as a marked Poisson point process on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, based on some Poisson point process
\xi_{\rm P}
on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, and a family
(B_{x})_{x\in\xi_{\rm P}}
of i.i.d. marks, given
\xi_{\rm P}
. The intensity of
\xi_{\rm P}
is

\overline{q}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mu}})}}=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{N}}q_{k}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mu}})}},\qquad\mbox{ with }\quad q_{k}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mu}})}}=\frac{{\rm e}^{\beta\mu k}}{(4\pi\beta)^{d/2}k^{1+d/2}},\qquad k\in\mathbb{N}.

(1.8)

Conditionally given
\xi_{\rm P}
, the length
\ell(B_{x})
is an
\mathbb{N}
-valued random variable with distribution
(q_{k}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mu}})}}/\overline{q}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mu}})}})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
, and, given
\ell(B_{x})=k
,
B_{x}
is in distribution equal to a Brownian bridge with time horizon
[0,k\beta]
, starting and ending at
x
. Let
\tt Q
denote the distribution of
\omega_{\rm P}
and denote by
\tt E
the corresponding expectation (respectively, write
\tt Q^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\rm bc}})}}
for
{\rm bc}\in\{{\rm per},{\rm Dir}\}
). Hence,
\tt Q
is a probability measure on the set
\Omega
of all locally finite counting measures on
\mathbb{R}^{d}\times E
. Note that our reference process is a countable superposition of Poisson point processes, and, as long as
\overline{q}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mu}})}}<\infty
is finite, this reference process is a Poisson point process as well. In accordance with the existence of the limiting pressure for the reference process, we consider
\mu\leq 0
in the following. Our other models allow positive values of the chemical potential
\mu\in\mathbb{R}
.

We now formulate our first main result, a presentation of the partition function defined in (
1.1
) in
\Lambda\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
with
|\Lambda|<\infty
and boundary condition
{\rm bc}\in\{\varnothing,{\rm per},{\rm Dir}\}
. We provide a general form of this representation for future reference. We introduce a functional on
\Omega
that expresses the interaction between particles in
\Lambda\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
, or more precisely, between their marks. Define the
Hamiltonian

H_{\Lambda}\colon\Omega\to[0,\infty]
by

H_{\Lambda}(\omega)=\sum_{x,y\in\xi\cap\Lambda}T_{x,y}(\omega),\qquad\mbox{where }\omega=\sum_{x\in\xi}\delta_{(x,f_{x})}\in\Omega,

(1.9)

where we abbreviate

T_{x,y}(\omega)=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i=0}^{\ell(f_{x})-1}\sum_{j=0}^{\ell(f_{y})-1}{\mathchoice{1\mskip-4.0mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-4.0mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-4.5mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-5.0mu\mathrm{l}}}_{\{(x,i)\not=(y,j)\}}\int_{0}^{\beta}v(|f_{x}(i\beta+s)-f_{y}(j\beta+s)|)\,{\rm d}s,\quad\omega\in\Omega,x,y\in\xi.

(1.10)

The function
H_{\Lambda}(\omega)
summarises the interaction between different marks of the point process and between different legs of the same mark; here we call the restriction of a mark
f_{x}
to the interval
[i\beta,(i+1)\beta)]
with
i\in\{0,\dots,\ell(f_{x})-1\}
a leg of the mark.
When
{\rm bc}\in\{{\rm per},{\rm Dir}\}
, we replace
\overline{q}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mu}})}}
and all
q_{k}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mu}})}}
by
\overline{q}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({{\rm bc},\mu}})}}
and
q_{k}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({{\rm bc},\mu}})}}
respectively, defined in (
A.4
) in Appendix
A
.

Proposition 1.1
.

Fix
\beta\in(0,\infty)
. Let
v\colon[0,\infty)\to(-\infty,\infty]
be measurable and bounded from below and let
\Lambda\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
be measurable with finite volume (assumed to be a torus for periodic boundary condition). Then, for any
{\rm bc}\in\{\varnothing,{\rm per},{\rm Dir}\}
,

\displaystyle Z_{\Lambda}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\rm bc}})}}(\beta,\mu)

\displaystyle={\rm e}^{\lvert\Lambda\rvert\overline{q}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({{\rm bc},\mu}})}}}{\tt E}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\rm bc}})}}\big[{\rm e}^{-H_{\Lambda}(\omega_{\rm P})}\}\big].

(1.11)

Formula (
1.11
) expresses the partition function as an expectation of the Boltzmann factor. The proof of Proposition
1.1
is a direct adaptation of the proof of
[
ACK11
, Proposition 1.1]
and is omitted here. This formula has also been recently obtained for Poisson loop processes on graphs, see
[
AV17
]
.

1.3.
Large deviations principles for empirical cycle counts

We let
\Lambda_{N}=[-N,N]^{d},N\in\mathbb{N}
, be a sequence of boxes
\Lambda_{N}\uparrow\mathbb{R}^{d}
as
N\to\infty
. We shall be interested in the countable set of random variables

\mathcal{N}_{k}(\omega)=\#\big\{x\in\xi\cap\Lambda_{N}\colon\ell(f_{x})=k\big\},\qquad k\in\mathbb{N},

(1.12)

being the number of points in
\Lambda_{N}
(whose marks do not have to be contained in
\Lambda_{N}
) with mark length equal to
k
.
These are independent Poisson random variables with respective means
\lvert\Lambda_{N}\rvert q^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mu}})}}_{k}
. The
empirical cycle count
is the empirical density of numbers of cycles of all lengths,

\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{N}(\omega)=\big(\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({k}})}}_{N}(\omega)\big)_{k\in\mathbb{N}}=\left(\frac{1}{\lvert\Lambda_{N}\rvert}\mathcal{N}_{k}\left(\omega\right)\right)_{k\in\mathbb{N}},

(1.13)

taking values in
\ell_{1}\left(\mathbb{R}_{+}\right)
.
Suppose

\left(\lambda_{k}\right)_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\in\mathcal{M}_{\Lambda_{N}}:=\Big\{\lambda\in\mathbb{R}^{\mathbb{N}}_{+}:\sum_{k\in\mathbb{N}}k\lambda_{k}<\infty;\left|\Lambda_{N}\right|\lambda_{k}\in\mathbb{N}_{0}\Big\}\subset\ell_{1}(\mathbb{R}_{+}).

(1.14)

Then, using the independence and the Poisson nature of our reference process, we compute the probability

{\tt Q}\big(\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{N}=(\lambda_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\big)=\prod_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\frac{{\rm e}^{-\lvert\Lambda_{N}\rvert q^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mu}})}}_{k}}\left(\lvert\Lambda_{N}\rvert q^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({{\rm bc},\mu}})}}_{k}\right)^{\lvert\Lambda_{N}\rvert\lambda_{k}}}{\left(\lvert\Lambda_{N}\rvert\lambda_{k}\right)!}.

(1.15)

The distribution on
\ell_{1}(\mathbb{R}_{+})
is denoted

\nu_{N,\mu}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({{\operatorname{bc}}}})}}:={\tt Q}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\rm bc}})}}\circ\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{-1}_{N},

which in fact has support
\mathcal{M}_{\Lambda_{N}}
. We shall derive large deviation principles for the empirical cycle counts under various measures. The result for
(\nu_{N,\mu})_{N\in\mathbb{N}}
is the case for the ideal Bose gas, that is, with vanishing interaction
v\equiv 0
.

Proposition 1.2

(
Ideal Bose gas, grand canonical ensemble
)
.

For
d\in\mathbb{N},\beta>0
,
\mu\leq 0
and
{\operatorname{bc}}\in\{\varnothing,{\text{\rm per}},{\operatorname{Dir}\,}\}
, the sequence
(\nu_{N,\mu}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({{\operatorname{bc}}}})}})_{N\in\mathbb{N}}
satisfies a large deviation principle (LDP) on
\ell_{1}(\mathbb{R})
with rate
\lvert\Lambda_{N}\rvert
and rate function

I_{\mu}\left(x\right)=\begin{cases}\sum^{\infty}_{k=1}x_{k}\left(\log\frac{x_{k}}{q^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mu}})}}_{k}}-1\right)+\bar{q}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mu}})}},&\text{for }x\in\ell_{1}\left(\mathbb{R}_{+}\right),\\
+\infty,&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

We shall now define our various interaction models. We restrict ourselves to interaction potentials which are functions of the empirical cycle counts. The first class are generalisations of mean-field models studied in the physics literature. In these models one adds an energy term proportional to the square of the particle/point number (density). The number of points for the reference process equals just the number of cycles and is just
N_{\Lambda_{N}}(\omega)=\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}{\mathcal{N}}_{k}(\omega)
. On the other hand, the number of “physical particles” is the total length of the marks of the particles in
\Lambda_{N}
,

N_{\Lambda_{N}}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\ell}})}}(\omega)=\sum_{x\in\xi\cap\Lambda_{N}}\ell(f_{x})=\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}k{\mathcal{N}}_{k}(\omega),

(1.16)

because a cycle with time horizon
k\beta
represents exactly
k
Boson particles. The functional
N_{\Lambda_{N}}
is continuous with respect to the reference process and continuous in the empirical cycle counts. The number of “physical particles” however, is only lower semicontinuous and not upper semicontinuous (see
[
ACK11
]
). For
a\geq 0
, define the cycle-mean-field model (CMF),

\displaystyle H^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{855497}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle CMF$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{855497}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle CMF$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{647441}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle CMF$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{647440}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle CMF$}}}}}}})}}(x)

\displaystyle=\frac{a}{2}\Big(\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}x_{k}\Big)^{2},\qquad x\in\ell_{1}(\mathbb{R}),

(1.17)

\displaystyle\nu_{N,\mu}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{855497}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle CMF$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{855497}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle CMF$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{647441}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle CMF$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{647440}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle CMF$}}}}}}})}}({\rm d}x)

\displaystyle=\frac{\exp(-\lvert\Lambda_{N}\rvert\beta H^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{855497}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle CMF$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{855497}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle CMF$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{647441}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle CMF$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{647440}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle CMF$}}}}}}})}}(x))}{Z_{N}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{855497}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle CMF$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{855497}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle CMF$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{647441}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle CMF$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{647440}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle CMF$}}}}}}})}}(\beta,\mu)}\nu_{N,\mu}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({{\operatorname{bc}}}})}}({\rm d}x),\quad\mu\leq 0,

with partition function

Z_{N}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{855497}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle CMF$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{855497}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle CMF$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{647441}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle CMF$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{647440}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle CMF$}}}}}}})}}(\beta,\mu)=\mathbb{E}_{\nu_{N,\mu}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({{\operatorname{bc}}}})}}}\big[{\rm e}^{-\lvert\Lambda_{N}\rvert\beta H^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{855497}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle CMF$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{855497}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle CMF$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{647441}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle CMF$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{647440}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle CMF$}}}}}}})}}(x)}\big]={\tt E}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({{\operatorname{bc}}}})}}\Big[\exp\big\{-\frac{\beta a}{2\lvert\Lambda_{N}\rvert}\big(N_{\Lambda_{N}})^{2}\big\}\Big],

and the particle-mean-field model (PMF),

\displaystyle H^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}}})}}_{\mu}(x)

\displaystyle=-\mu\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}kx_{k}+\frac{a}{2}\Big(\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}kx_{k}\Big)^{2},\qquad x\in\ell_{1}(\mathbb{R}),

(1.18)

\displaystyle\nu_{N,\mu,\alpha}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{851811}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle PMF$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{851811}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle PMF$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646134}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle PMF$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle PMF$}}}}}}})}}({\rm d}x)

\displaystyle=\frac{\exp(-\lvert\Lambda_{N}\rvert\beta H^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}}})}}_{\mu}(x))}{Z_{N}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}}})}}(\beta,\mu,\alpha)}\nu_{N,\alpha}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({{\operatorname{bc}}}})}}({\rm d}x),\quad\alpha\leq 0,\mu\in\mathbb{R},

with partition function

\displaystyle Z_{N}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}}})}}(\beta,\mu,\alpha)=\mathbb{E}_{\nu_{N,\alpha}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({{\operatorname{bc}}}})}}}\big[{\rm e}^{-\lvert\Lambda_{N}\rvert\beta H^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}}})}}_{\mu}(x)}\big]={\tt E}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({{\operatorname{bc}}}})}}\Big[\exp\big\{\beta\mu N_{\Lambda}^{\ell}-\frac{\beta a}{2\lvert\Lambda_{N}\rvert}\big(N_{\Lambda_{N}}^{\ell}\big)^{2}\big\}\Big].

The following large deviation principles hold.

Remark 1.5
.

For

reads

I^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}}})}}_{\mu,\alpha}(x)=\beta H^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}}})}}_{\mu+\alpha}(x)+I(x)-\inf_{y\in\ell_{1}\left(\mathbb{R}\right)}\{\beta H^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}}})}}_{\mu+\alpha}(y)+I(y)\}.

\diamond

The major novelty of our large deviation analysis concerns an substantial extension of the so-called HYL-model (Hunag-Yang-Luttinger-model) studied in
[
BLP88
]
. On one hand we replace the cycle weights originating from the energy representation in
[
BLP88
]
by our cycle weights stemming from spatial representation of the partition function, and on the other hand we obtain higher level large deviation principles allowing a detailed insight in the structure of the minimiser and possible phases and phase transitions.
For any
a\geq b>0
and any
\alpha\leq 0,\mu\in\mathbb{R}
, define the HYL-model by

\displaystyle H^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{792963}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle HYL$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{792963}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle HYL$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{585927}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle HYL$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{585928}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle HYL$}}}}}}})}_{\mu}(x)

\displaystyle=-\mu\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}kx_{k}+\frac{a}{2}\Big(\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}kx_{k}\Big)^{2}-\frac{b}{2}\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}k^{2}x_{k}^{2},\qquad x\in\ell_{1}(\mathbb{R}_{+}),

(1.22)

\displaystyle\nu^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{792963}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle HYL$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{792963}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle HYL$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{585927}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle HYL$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{585928}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle HYL$}}}}}}})}_{N,\mu,\alpha}({\rm d}x)

\displaystyle=\frac{\exp(-\beta\lvert\Lambda_{N}\rvert H^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{792963}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle HYL$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{792963}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle HYL$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{585927}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle HYL$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{585928}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle HYL$}}}}}}})}_{\mu}(x))}{Z^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{792963}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle HYL$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{792963}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle HYL$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{585927}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle HYL$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{585928}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle HYL$}}}}}}})}_{N}(\beta,\mu,\alpha)}\,\nu_{N,\alpha}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({{\operatorname{bc}}}})}}({\rm d}x),

with partition function

\displaystyle Z_{N}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{792963}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle HYL$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{792963}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle HYL$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{585927}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle HYL$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{585928}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle HYL$}}}}}}})}}(\beta,\mu,\alpha)=\mathbb{E}_{\nu_{N,\alpha}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({{\operatorname{bc}}}})}}}\big[{\rm e}^{-\lvert\Lambda_{N}\rvert\beta H^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{792963}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle HYL$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{792963}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle HYL$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{585927}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle HYL$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{585928}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle HYL$}}}}}}})}}_{\mu}(x)}\big]={\tt E}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({{\operatorname{bc}}}})}}\big[\exp\big\{\beta\mu N_{\Lambda}^{\ell}-\frac{\beta a}{2\lvert\Lambda_{N}\rvert}\big(N_{\Lambda}^{\ell}\big)^{2}+\frac{\beta b}{2\lvert\Lambda_{N}\rvert}\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}k^{2}{\mathcal{N}}_{k}^{2}\big\}\big].

In this paper we will focus our attention on the
a>b
case. For a discussion of
a=b
, see
[
AD18b
]
.

(1.23)

with

\displaystyle H^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{792963}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle HYL$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{792963}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle HYL$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{585927}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle HYL$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{585928}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle HYL$}}}}}}})}}_{\mu,l.s.c.}(x)

\displaystyle=H^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{792963}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle HYL$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{792963}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle HYL$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{585927}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle HYL$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{585928}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle HYL$}}}}}}})}(x)-\frac{1}{2(a-b)}\big(\mu-aD(x)\big)_{+}^{2}

(1.24)

\displaystyle=-\frac{b}{2}\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}k^{2}x_{k}^{2}+\begin{cases}-\mu D(x)+\frac{a}{2}D(x)^{2}&,D(x)\geq\frac{\mu}{a},\\
-\frac{b}{a-b}\left(-\mu D(x)+\frac{a}{2}D(x)^{2}\right)-\frac{\mu^{2}}{2(a-b)}&,D(x)<\frac{\mu}{a}.\end{cases}

Remark 1.7
.

In the PMF and HYL models we have made a distinction between the chemical potential arising in the reference measure,
\alpha
, and the chemical potential arising in the relevant Hamiltonian,
\mu
. Because the ideal Bose gas partition function diverges for
\alpha>0
, we require
\alpha\leq 0
, but no such bound is needed for
\mu
. Note though, that a model with
\alpha=\alpha_{1}\leq 0
and
\mu=\mu_{1}\in\mathbb{R}
coincides with same model but with
\alpha=0
and
\mu=\mu_{1}+\alpha_{1}
. In many of our proofs we will - without loss of generality - use the
\alpha=0
and
\mu=\mu_{1}+\alpha_{1}
arrangement.
\diamond

Following
[
BLP88
]
and
[
Lew86
]
one might be interested in large deviation principles for the empirical particle density

\boldsymbol{\rho}_{N}:=\frac{1}{\lvert\Lambda_{N}\rvert}N_{\Lambda_{N}}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\ell}})}}=\frac{1}{\lvert\Lambda_{N}\rvert}\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}k{\mathcal{N}}_{k}.

(1.25)

As the particle number
N_{\Lambda_{N}}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\ell}})}}
is only lower semicontinuous and not upper semicontinuous, a proof via the contraction principle is only feasible if one considers cut-off versions of the empirical particle density
\boldsymbol{\rho}_{N}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({K}})}}=\frac{1}{\lvert\Lambda_{N}\rvert}\sum^{K}_{k=1}k{\mathcal{N}}_{k}
followed by analysing the limit
K\to\infty
for the corresponding rate functions. We do not follow this approach here and briefly outline a direct approach as follows.

Proposition 1.8
.

Let
\alpha\leq 0
, then for all
t\in\mathbb{R}
,

\displaystyle\Lambda(t)

\displaystyle:=\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{1}{\lvert\Lambda_{N}\rvert}\log\mathbb{E}_{\nu_{N,\alpha}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({{\operatorname{bc}}}})}}}\Big[{\rm e}^{t\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}k\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{N}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({k}})}}}\Big]=\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{1}{\lvert\Lambda_{N}\rvert}\log{\tt E}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({{\operatorname{bc}}}})}}\Big[{\rm e}^{t\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}k{\mathcal{N}}_{k}}\Big]

(1.26)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}q_{k}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\alpha}})}}\big({\rm e}^{tk}-1\big)=\begin{cases}+\infty&,\mbox{ if }t>\lvert\alpha\rvert\beta,\\
\in\mathbb{R}&,\mbox{ if }\alpha\beta+t\leq 0.\end{cases}

The following large deviation results uses the critical density for the ideal Bose gas, the thermodynamic limit of the pressure and the free energy defined, respectively, in (
2.12
) in Section
2.2.1
, (
2.7
) in Section
2.6
and Proposition
2.11
in Section
2.2.1
. Denote
Q_{N,\alpha}={\tt Q}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({{\operatorname{bc}}}})}}\circ\boldsymbol{\rho}_{N}^{-1}
the distribution of
(\boldsymbol{\rho}_{N})_{N\geq 1}
with chemical potential
\alpha\leq 0
and define the distribution
Q_{N,\mu,\alpha}^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}}})}
via its Radon-Nikodym density

\frac{{\rm d}Q_{N,\mu,\alpha}^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}}})}}{{\rm d}Q_{N,\alpha}}(x)=\frac{\exp\big(-\beta\big(-\mu x+\frac{a}{2}x^{2}\big)\big)}{Z_{N}^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}}})}(\beta,\mu,\alpha)}.

(1.27)

Theorem 1.9
.

Let
d\in\mathbb{N},\beta>0
, and
{\operatorname{bc}}\in\{\varnothing,{\text{\rm per}},{\operatorname{Dir}\,}\}
.

(1)

For any
\alpha<0
, the sequence
\big(Q_{N,\alpha}\big)_{N\geq 1}
satisfies an LDP on
\mathbb{R}
with rate
\lvert\Lambda_{N}\rvert
and rate function

J_{\alpha}(x)=\begin{cases}\beta\big(p(\beta,\alpha)+f(\beta,x)-\alpha x\big)&,\mbox{ if }x\in[0,\varrho_{\rm c}]\;\mbox{ for }d\geq 3\wedge\;x\in[0,\infty)\;\mbox{ for }d=1,2,\\
+\infty&,\mbox{ if }x\notin[0,\varrho_{\rm c}].\end{cases}

(1.28)

(2)

For any
\alpha<0
and
\mu\in\mathbb{R}
, the sequence
\big(Q_{N,\mu,\alpha}^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}}})}\big)_{N\geq 1}
satisfies an LDP on
\mathbb{R}
with rate
\lvert\Lambda_{N}\rvert
and rate function

J_{\mu,\alpha}^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}}})}(x)=\begin{cases}\beta(-\mu x+\frac{a}{2}x^{2})+J_{\alpha}(x)-{\mathcal{N}}&,\mbox{ if }x\in[0,\varrho_{\rm c}]\;\mbox{ for }d\geq 3\wedge\;x\in[0,\infty)\;\mbox{ for }d=1,2,\\
+\infty&,\mbox{ if }x\notin[0,\varrho_{\rm c}],\end{cases}

(1.29)

where
{\mathcal{N}}=\inf_{y\in\mathbb{R}}\{\beta(-\mu y+\frac{a}{2}y^{2})+J_{\alpha}(y)\}
.

Remark 1.10
.

The results in Theorem
1.9
make the heuristic derivations in
[
Lew86
]
rigorous and extend them to all
\alpha<0
and
\mu\in\mathbb{R}
.
The free energy of the PMF model is
f^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}}})}(\beta,\varrho)=f(\beta,\varrho)+\frac{a}{2}\varrho^{2}
, whereas the pressure

\displaystyle p^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}}})}(\beta,\mu,\alpha)

\displaystyle=p(\beta,\alpha)+\frac{1}{\beta}\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}}\big\{-\beta h(x)-J_{\alpha}(x)\big\}=\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}}\big\{(\mu+\alpha)x-\frac{a}{2}x^{2}-f(\beta,x)\big\}

(1.30)

\displaystyle=\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}}\big\{(\mu+\alpha)x-f^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}}})}(\beta,x)\big\},

with
h(x):=-\mu x+\frac{a}{2}x^{2}
.
The CMF and the HYL model both require higher level empirical functionals as the energy cannot be expressed as a functional of the empirical particle density.

\diamond

2.
Variational analysis, pressure representations, and BEC

Our large deviation analysis in Section
1.3
is complemented by a complete analysis for the zeroes of the rate functions in Section
2.1
followed by pressure representations in Section
2.2
. In Section
2.3
, we finally study the onset of Bose-Einstein condensation (BEC) and discuss the relevance of our results.

2.1.
Zeroes of the Rate Functions

We have shown that the ideal Bose gas model, the cycle mean-field (CMF) model, and the particle mean-field (PMF) model and the hard-sphere (HYL) model all satisfy a LDP for empirical cycle counts with the rate functions
I_{\mu}
,
I^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{855497}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle CMF$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{855497}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle CMF$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{647441}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle CMF$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{647440}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle CMF$}}}}}}})}}_{\mu}
,
I^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}}})}}_{\mu,\alpha}
, and
I^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{792963}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle HYL$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{792963}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle HYL$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{585927}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle HYL$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{585928}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle HYL$}}}}}}})}_{\mu,\alpha}
respectively.

We summarise our results on the zeroes in the following statements.

Proposition 2.1
.

The rate function for the ideal Bose gas model,
I_{\mu}
, has a unique zero
\xi\in\ell_{1}\left(\mathbb{R}\right)
given by

\xi_{k}=q^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mu}})}}_{k},\quad k\in\mathbb{N}.

(2.1)

Proposition 2.2
.

The rate function
I^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{855497}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle CMF$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{855497}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle CMF$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{647441}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle CMF$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{647440}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle CMF$}}}}}}})}}_{\mu}
has a unique zero at
\xi^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{855497}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle CMF$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{855497}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle CMF$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{647441}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle CMF$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{647440}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle CMF$}}}}}}})}\in\ell_{1}\left(\mathbb{R}\right)
given by

\xi^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{855497}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle CMF$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{855497}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle CMF$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{647441}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle CMF$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{647440}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle CMF$}}}}}}})}_{k}=\frac{W_{0}\left(K\right)}{K}q^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mu}})}}_{k},\quad k\in\mathbb{N},

(2.2)

where
W_{0}
is the real branch of the Lambert W function for non-negative arguments, and
K=K\left(a,\beta,\mu\right)
is a dimensionless quantity defined by

K:=a\beta\bar{q}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mu}})}}=\frac{a\beta}{\left(4\pi\beta\right)^{\frac{d}{2}}}g\left(1+\frac{d}{2},-\beta\mu\right).

(2.3)

Remark 2.3
.

Definition and properties of the Lambert function are given in Appendix
C
.

\diamond

Proposition 2.4
.

The rate function
I^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}}})}_{\mu,\alpha}
has a unique zero at
\xi^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}}})}\in\ell_{1}(\mathbb{R}_{+})
where

\xi^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}}})}_{k}=q_{k}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({0}})}}\exp\big(\beta k\big(\mu+\alpha-a\delta^{*}\big)_{-}\big),\quad k\in\mathbb{N},

and
\delta^{*}=\delta^{*}(\beta,\mu+\alpha,a)
is given implicitly as the unique solution to the equation

\delta^{*}=\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}kq_{k}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mu}})}}\exp\big(\beta k\big(\mu+\alpha-a\delta^{*}\big)_{-}\big).

(2.4)

Remark 2.5
.

Note that Proposition
2.4
tells us that the zero of
I^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}}})}_{\mu,\alpha}
is equal to the zero of
I_{\eta}
, where
\eta=\left(\mu+\alpha-a\delta^{*}\right)_{-}
.
\diamond

The zeroes for the HYL model are more complex and involved. We summarise this in the following two propositions.

Proposition 2.6
.

The zeroes
\xi\subset\ell_{1}(\mathbb{R}_{+})
of rate the function
I^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{792963}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle HYL$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{792963}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle HYL$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{585927}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle HYL$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{585928}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle HYL$}}}}}}})}_{\mu,\alpha}
satisfy the following expression,

\xi_{k}=-\frac{1}{b\beta k^{2}}W_{\chi^{*}_{k}}\left(-b\beta k^{2}q_{k}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({0}})}}\exp\left[\beta k\left(\mu+\alpha-a\delta^{*}\right)\begin{Bmatrix}1&:a\delta^{*}\geq\mu+\alpha\\
-\frac{b}{a-b}&:a\delta^{*}\leq\mu+\alpha\end{Bmatrix}\right]\right),\qquad k\in\mathbb{N},

where
\left(\delta^{*},\chi^{*}\right)\in\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\left\{0,-1\right\}^{\mathbb{N}}
is a solution to

\delta=g^{\chi}\left(\delta\right):=-\frac{1}{b\beta}\sum^{\infty}_{j=1}\frac{1}{j}W_{\chi_{j}}\left(-b\beta j^{2}q^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({0}})}}_{j}\exp\left[\beta j\left(\mu+\alpha-a\delta\right)\begin{Bmatrix}1&:a\delta\geq\mu+\alpha\\
-\frac{b}{a-b}&:a\delta\leq\mu+\alpha\end{Bmatrix}\right]\right).

(2.5)

The next proposition shows that there exists a regime for the parameters
\beta,\alpha,\mu,a,b,d
such that the rate function has a unique zero.

Proposition 2.7
.

There exists
\tilde{\mu}=\tilde{\mu}\left(d,\beta,a,b\right)\in\mathbb{R}
such that for
\mu+\alpha<\tilde{\mu}
the rate function
I^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{792963}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle HYL$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{792963}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle HYL$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{585927}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle HYL$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{585928}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle HYL$}}}}}}})}_{\mu,\alpha}
has a unique zero at
\xi^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{792963}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle HYL$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{792963}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle HYL$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{585927}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle HYL$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{585928}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle HYL$}}}}}}})}\in\ell_{1}\left(\mathbb{R}_{+}\right)
where

\xi^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{792963}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle HYL$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{792963}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle HYL$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{585927}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle HYL$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{585928}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle HYL$}}}}}}})}_{k}=-\frac{1}{b\beta k^{2}}W_{0}\left(-b\beta k^{2}q_{k}^{\scriptscriptstyle{({0}})}\exp\left[\beta k\left(\mu+\alpha-a\delta^{*}\right)\right]\right),\qquad k\in\mathbb{N},

and
\delta^{*}=\delta^{*}(\beta,\mu+\alpha,a,b)
is given implicitly as the unique solution to the equation
\delta^{*}=g^{0}\left(\delta^{*}\right)
,
where
g^{0}=g^{\chi\equiv 0}
. See Figure
1
.

0
g^{0}\left(\delta\right)
\delta
\frac{\mu+\alpha}{a}

Figure 1
.
Sketch of
g^{\chi}\left(\delta\right)
for

,
d=3,4
, and
\beta\leq\left(\frac{b^{2}{\rm e}^{2}}{\left(4\pi\right)^{d}}\right)^{\frac{1}{d-2}}
. This shows

, but the sketch translates with
