.

2.2.
Pressure and Bose-Einstein condensation

We derive representations for the pressure and use their formulae to prove the onset of a phase transition called Bose-Einstein condensation in all our models.
Using our large deviation principles in Section
1.3
and the zeroes of the rate functions in conjunction with our representation of the partition functions in Proposition
1.1
we obtain the thermodynamic limit of the pressure in our various models, defined as

p^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\rm bc}})}}(\beta,\mu):=\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{1}{\beta\lvert\Lambda_{N}\rvert}\log Z_{\Lambda_{N}}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\rm bc}})}}(\beta,\mu).

(2.6)

We summarise our findings in the following theorem.

Theorem 2.8

(
Pressure representations
)
.

Let
\beta>0
and
{\rm bc}\in\{\varnothing,{\text{\rm per}},{\operatorname{Dir}\,}\}
, then

\displaystyle p(\beta,\mu)

\displaystyle=\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{1}{\beta\lvert\Lambda_{N}\rvert}\log{\rm e}^{\lvert\Lambda_{N}\rvert\overline{q}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\rm bc}})},\mu}}=\frac{1}{\beta(4\pi\beta)^{\frac{d}{2}}}\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\frac{{\rm e}^{\beta\mu k}}{k^{1+d/2}},\qquad\text{with }\mu\leq 0,

(2.7)

\displaystyle p^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{855497}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle CMF$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{855497}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle CMF$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{647441}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle CMF$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{647440}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle CMF$}}}}}}})}(\beta,\mu)

\displaystyle=\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{1}{\beta\lvert\Lambda_{N}\rvert}\log Z_{N}^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{855497}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle CMF$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{855497}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle CMF$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{647441}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle CMF$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{647440}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle CMF$}}}}}}})}(\beta,\mu)=\frac{1}{a\beta^{2}}W_{0}\left(a\beta\overline{q}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mu}})}}\right)\Big(1+\frac{1}{2}W_{0}\big(a\beta\overline{q}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mu}})}}\big)\Big),

\displaystyle\quad\text{ with }\mu\leq 0,\text{and }a\geq 0,

(2.8)

\displaystyle p^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}}})}(\beta,\mu,\alpha)

\displaystyle=\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{1}{\beta\lvert\Lambda_{N}\rvert}\log Z_{N}^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}}})}(\beta,\mu,\alpha)

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\frac{a}{2}{\delta^{*}}^{2}+\frac{1}{\beta}\sum^{\infty}_{k=1}q^{\scriptscriptstyle{({0}})}_{k}\exp\left(\beta k\left(\mu+\alpha-a\delta^{*}\right)\right)&,\mu+\alpha\leq a\varrho_{\mathrm{c}},\\[6.45831pt]
\frac{\left(\mu+\alpha\right)^{2}}{2a}+\frac{1}{\beta}\sum^{\infty}_{k=1}q^{\scriptscriptstyle{({0}})}_{k}&,\mu+\alpha\geq a\varrho_{\mathrm{c}},\end{cases}

\displaystyle\quad\text{ with }\mu\in\mathbb{R},\alpha\leq 0,\text{and }a\geq 0,

(2.9)

\displaystyle p^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{792963}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle HYL$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{792963}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle HYL$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{585927}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle HYL$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{585928}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle HYL$}}}}}}})}(\beta,\mu,\alpha)

\displaystyle=\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{1}{\beta\lvert\Lambda_{N}\rvert}\log Z_{N}^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{792963}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle HYL$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{792963}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle HYL$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{585927}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle HYL$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{585928}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle HYL$}}}}}}})}(\beta,\mu,\alpha)=p\left(\beta,0\right)-\inf_{x\in\ell_{1}(\mathbb{R})}\left\{I(x)+\beta H^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{792963}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle HYL$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{792963}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle HYL$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{585927}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle HYL$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{585928}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle HYL$}}}}}}})}_{\mu+\alpha,l.s.c.}(x)\right\},

\displaystyle\quad\text{ with }\mu\in\mathbb{R},\alpha\leq 0,\text{and }a>b\geq 0.

(2.10)

Proof of Theorem
2.8
.
The thermodynamic limit of the average finite-volume pressure exists in all models, see
[
BR97
]
. The independence of the thermodynamic limit on the choice of boundary conditions follows with
[
ACK11
]
, or using
[
Rob71
,
AN73
]
, see Appendix
A
.
The pressure for the ideal Bose gas in (
2.7
) follows easily using (
1.11
) and the independence of the boundary conditions.

For (
2.8
), (
2.9
), and (
2.10
) our large deviation principles give us

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{1}{\left|\Lambda_{N}\right|}\log Z^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{855497}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle CMF$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{855497}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle CMF$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{647441}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle CMF$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{647440}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle CMF$}}}}}}})}_{N}(\beta,\mu)

\displaystyle=p\left(\beta,\mu\right)-\inf_{x\in\ell_{1}(\mathbb{R})}\left\{I_{\mu}(x)+\beta H^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{855497}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle CMF$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{855497}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle CMF$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{647441}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle CMF$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{647440}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle CMF$}}}}}}})}(x)\right\},

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{1}{\left|\Lambda_{N}\right|}\log Z^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}}})}_{N}(\beta,\mu,\alpha)

\displaystyle=p\left(\beta,0\right)-\inf_{x\in\ell_{1}(\mathbb{R})}\left\{I(x)+\beta H^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}}})}_{\mu+\alpha,l.s.c.}(x)\right\},

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{1}{\left|\Lambda_{N}\right|}\log Z^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{792963}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle HYL$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{792963}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle HYL$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{585927}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle HYL$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{585928}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle HYL$}}}}}}})}_{N}(\beta,\mu,\alpha)

\displaystyle=p\left(\beta,0\right)-\inf_{x\in\ell_{1}(\mathbb{R})}\left\{I(x)+\beta H^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{792963}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle HYL$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{792963}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle HYL$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{585927}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle HYL$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{585928}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle HYL$}}}}}}})}_{\mu+\alpha,l.s.c.}(x)\right\}.

Then for (
2.8
) and (
2.9
) we substitute in the zeroes found in Proposition
2.2
and Proposition
2.4
. The ideal Bose gas pressure
p(\beta,\mu)
respectively
p(\beta,\alpha)
appears in all pressure formulae due to the term in (
1.11
) stemming from the reference Poisson process. ∎

In the following subsections we study our different models separately in terms of their thermodynamic behaviour and the onset of Bose-Einstein condensation. Crucial observation is that the partial derivative of the pressure with respect to the chemical potential
\mu
at inverse temperature
\beta
equals the expected physical particle density at equilibrium in the grand canonical ensemble at inverse temperature
\beta
and chemical potential
\mu
. In the following, we will distinguish between different regimes depending on whether the expected particle density equals the density of the zeroes of the rate functions or not. In the latter case, the excess density equals the density of the condensate in the BEC state.

2.2.1.
Thermodynamics and BEC of the ideal Bose gas

We collect well-known properties of the ideal gas pressure for convenience of the reader and comparison purposes to our other models, for more details see
[
BLP88
]
and
[
BCMP05
]
.

Proposition 2.9
.

(1)

For
\beta>0,\mu>0
, we define
p(\beta,\mu)=+\infty
. Then the pressure
p(\beta,\cdot)
is a closed convex function on
\mathbb{R}
.

(2)

For
\beta>0,\mu<0
, the ideal gas pressure
p(\beta,\mu)
is smooth with respect to
\mu
. In particular,

\frac{{\rm d}p}{{\rm d}\mu}=D\left(q^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mu}})}}\right).

We denote

p^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\rm bc}})}}_{\Lambda_{N}}=\frac{1}{\beta\lvert\Lambda_{N}\rvert}Z_{\Lambda_{N}}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\rm bc}})}}(\beta,\mu)

the average finite-volume pressure with
{\operatorname{bc}}\in\{\varnothing,{\text{\rm per}},{\operatorname{Dir}\,}\}
.

Proposition 2.10
.

(1)

For
\beta>0,\mu<0
, and any
N\in\mathbb{N}
,

\frac{1}{\lvert\Lambda_{N}\rvert}{\tt E}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\rm bc}})}}\big[N_{\Lambda_{N}}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\ell}})}}\big]=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{N}}kq_{k}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({{\rm bc},\mu}})}}=\frac{{\rm d}}{{\rm d}\mu}p_{\Lambda_{N}}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\rm bc}})}}(\beta,\mu).

(2.11)

The function
\mu\mapsto\frac{{\rm d}}{{\rm d}\mu}p_{\Lambda_{N}}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\rm bc}})}}(\beta,\mu)
is increasing on
(-\infty,0)
. It follows that we can give
\frac{1}{\lvert\Lambda_{N}\rvert}{\tt E}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\rm bc}})}}[N_{\Lambda_{N}}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\ell}})}}]
any pre-assigned value
\varrho\in(0,\infty)
by choosing
\mu_{N}(\varrho)\in(-\infty,0)
.

(2)

In the thermodynamic limit
N\to\infty
,

\varrho_{\rm c}:=\lim_{\mu\uparrow 0}\big(\frac{{\rm d}}{{\rm d}\mu}p^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\rm bc}})}}_{\Lambda_{N}}(\beta,\mu)\big)=\begin{cases}+\infty&,d=1,2,\\
\frac{1}{\left(4\pi\beta\right)^{\frac{d}{2}}}\zeta\left(\frac{d}{2}\right)&,d\geq 3.\end{cases}

(2.12)

Let
\mu_{N}(\varrho)
denote the unique root of

\big(\frac{{\rm d}}{{\rm d}\mu}p^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\rm bc}})}}_{\Lambda_{N}}(\beta,\mu)\big)=\varrho

(2.13)

then
\mu(\varrho)=\lim_{N\to\infty}\mu_{N}(\varrho)
exists and is equal to the unique root of

\big(\frac{{\rm d}}{{\rm d}\mu}p^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\rm bc}})}}(\beta,\mu)\big)=\varrho\quad\mbox{ if }\varrho<\varrho_{\rm c},

(2.14)

and it is equal to zero otherwise.

Proposition 2.11
.

For
\varrho>0
, we define the ideal Bose gas free energy as the Legendre-Fenchel transform of the pressure,

\displaystyle f\left(\beta,\varrho\right):=\sup_{\mu\in\mathbb{R}}\left\{\mu\varrho-p\left(\beta,\mu\right)\right\}=\begin{cases}\frac{-1}{\beta\left(4\pi\beta\right)^{\frac{d}{2}}}g(1+\frac{d}{2},-\beta\alpha)+\varrho\alpha&,\varrho\leq\varrho_{\rm c}\;,\\
\frac{-1}{\beta\left(4\pi\beta\right)^{\frac{d}{2}}}\zeta\left(1+\frac{d}{2}\right)&,\varrho\geq\varrho_{\rm c}\;,\end{cases}

(2.15)

where
\alpha\leq 0
is a solution to

\frac{1}{\left(4\pi\beta\right)^{\frac{d}{2}}}g\big(\frac{d}{2},-\beta\alpha\big)=\varrho,

which exists and is unique for
\varrho\leq\varrho_{\rm c}
.

It is easy to see that
\varrho\mapsto f(\beta,\varrho)
is a decreasing convex function; it is given by

f(\beta,\varrho)=\mu(\varrho)\varrho-p(\beta,\mu(\varrho))\quad\mbox{ for }\varrho<\varrho_{\rm c}.

The linear segment in the graph of
f
where
f
is constant and equal to
-p(\beta,0)
for
\varrho\geq\varrho_{\rm c}
, signals a first-order phase-transition at
\mu=0
. This phase-transition is called Bose-Einstein condensation (BEC).

In
[
Lew86
]
, Lewis suggests an order parameter for the Bose-Einstein condensation phase transition. Having in mind the density of particles with zero single particle energy in the thermodynamic limit, Lewis first takes the finite volume expected density of particles with energy below some cut-off. He then takes the thermodynamic limit before taking the cut-off to zero. In contrast to Lewis’ model, we do not keep track of the particles’ energy. Instead, we partition our gas by loop type, and expect the condensate to occupy loops of diverging length. Therefore we want to evaluate the ‘condensate density’ given by

\Delta\left(\beta,\mu\right):=\lim_{K\to\infty}\lim_{N\to\infty}\mathbb{E}_{\nu_{N,\mu}}\left[D-D_{K}\right],\qquad D_{K}\left(x\right):=\sum^{K}_{j=1}jx_{j}.

Theorem 2.12
.

For
\beta>0
,
\mu<0
, we have
\Delta\left(\beta,\mu\right)=0
.

For
\beta>0
,
\mu=0
,

\Delta\left(\beta,0\right)=\begin{cases}+\infty&,d=1,2,\text{ or }d\geq 3,{\operatorname{bc}}={\rm per}\\
0&,d\geq 3,{\operatorname{bc}}\in\left\{\varnothing,{\rm Dir}\right\}.\end{cases}

Conclusion.
The ideal gas shows some critical behaviour, namely, the density and chemical potential relation breaks down in dimensions
d\geq 3
. This can be seen as a signal of a phase transition manifesting itself in the in-equivalence of the canonical and grand canonical ensemble. The excess density above the critical value of the density is identified as the BEC condensate density. The free energy is constant for all densities above the critical one, showing that the condensate density does not contribute to the free energy. In
[
BCMP05
]
, using the energy (Fourier) representation, it is shown that the excess density (for dimensions
d\geq 3
) equals the expected density of particles in the zero-energy mode. Mathematically, the critical behaviour is seen in the thermodynamic limit of distribution function of the empirical particle density (called Kac distribution), the limit of the Kac distribution exists only for densities
\varrho<\varrho_{\rm c}
and is the degenerate distribution

\mathbb{K}(x)=\begin{cases}0&,x<\varrho,\\
1&,x\geq\varrho.\end{cases}

This critical behaviour is also seen in the rate function for the density large deviation principle in Theorem
1.9
. We believe that the unexpected results in Theorem
2.12
are due to this degeneracy.

2.2.2.
Thermodynamics of the CMF model

We summarise our findings for the CMF model below.

Proposition 2.13
.

(1)

For
\beta>0
, we define
p^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{855497}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle CMF$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{855497}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle CMF$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{647441}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle CMF$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{647440}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle CMF$}}}}}}})}(\beta,\mu)=+\infty
for
\mu>0
. Then
p^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{855497}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle CMF$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{855497}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle CMF$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{647441}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle CMF$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{647440}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle CMF$}}}}}}})}(\beta,\cdot)
is a closed convex function on
\mathbb{R}
.

(2)

For
\beta>0
,
\mu<0
, the CMF gas pressure
p^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{855497}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle CMF$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{855497}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle CMF$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{647441}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle CMF$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{647440}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle CMF$}}}}}}})}(\beta,\mu)
is smooth with respect to
\mu
. In particular,

\frac{{\rm d}p^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{855497}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle CMF$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{855497}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle CMF$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{647441}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle CMF$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{647440}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle CMF$}}}}}}})}}{{\rm d}\mu}=D\left(\xi^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{855497}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle CMF$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{855497}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle CMF$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{647441}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle CMF$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{647440}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle CMF$}}}}}}})}\right)=\frac{W_{0}\left(a\beta\bar{q}^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mu}})}\right)}{a\beta\bar{q}^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mu}})}}D\left(q^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mu}})}\right).

(3)

In the thermodynamic limit
N\to\infty
,

\varrho^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{855497}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle CMF$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{855497}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle CMF$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{647441}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle CMF$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{647440}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle CMF$}}}}}}})}}_{\rm c}:=\lim_{\mu\uparrow 0}\big(\frac{{\rm d}}{{\rm d}\mu}p^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{855497}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle CMF$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{855497}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle CMF$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{647441}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle CMF$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{647440}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle CMF$}}}}}}})}}_{\Lambda_{N}}(\beta,\mu)\big)=\begin{cases}+\infty&,d=1,2,\\[6.45831pt]
\frac{W_{0}(a\beta\overline{q}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({0}})}})}{a\beta\overline{q}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({0}})}}(4\pi\beta)^{\frac{d}{2}}}\zeta\big(\frac{d}{2}\big)&,d\geq 3.\end{cases}

(2.16)

Proposition 2.14
.

For
\varrho>0
, the CMF Bose gas free energy is defined as the Legendre-Fenchel transform of the pressure,

f^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{855497}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle CMF$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{855497}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle CMF$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{647441}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle CMF$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{647440}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle CMF$}}}}}}})}\left(\beta,\varrho\right):=\sup_{\alpha\in\mathbb{R}}\left\{\alpha\varrho-p^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{855497}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle CMF$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{855497}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle CMF$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{647441}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle CMF$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{647440}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle CMF$}}}}}}})}\left(\beta,\alpha\right)\right\}=\begin{cases}\varrho\mu-p^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{855497}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle CMF$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{855497}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle CMF$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{647441}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle CMF$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{647440}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle CMF$}}}}}}})}\left(\beta,\mu\right)&,\varrho\leq\varrho^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{855497}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle CMF$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{855497}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle CMF$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{647441}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle CMF$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{647440}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle CMF$}}}}}}})}_{\rm c},\\
-p^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{855497}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle CMF$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{855497}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle CMF$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{647441}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle CMF$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{647440}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle CMF$}}}}}}})}\left(\beta,0\right)&,\varrho\geq\varrho^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{855497}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle CMF$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{855497}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle CMF$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{647441}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle CMF$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{647440}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle CMF$}}}}}}})}_{\rm c},\end{cases}

(2.17)

where
\alpha=\mu
is a solution to

\frac{1}{a\beta\bar{q}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\alpha}})}}}W_{0}\left(a\beta\bar{q}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\alpha}})}}\right)\sum^{\infty}_{k=1}kq_{k}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\alpha}})}}=\varrho,

which exists and is unique for
\varrho\leq\varrho^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{855497}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle CMF$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{855497}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle CMF$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{647441}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle CMF$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{647440}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle CMF$}}}}}}})}_{\rm c}:=\frac{W_{0}(K)}{K}\varrho_{\rm c}
.

Theorem 2.15
.

For all
\beta>0
,
\mu<0
,

\Delta^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{855497}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle CMF$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{855497}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle CMF$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{647441}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle CMF$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{647440}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle CMF$}}}}}}})}\left(\beta,\mu\right)=0.

Conclusion.
The CMF model shows the same BEC phase transition as the ideal Bose gas as the free energy is constant in the density beyond its specific critical density. The critical density of the CMF model is different from the ideal Bose gas one. Using properties of the Lambert function, see Appendix
C
, we know that

\displaystyle\lim_{c\downarrow 0}\frac{W_{0}(cx)}{cx}

\displaystyle=1,

\displaystyle\lim_{c\to\infty}\frac{W_{0}(cx)}{cx}

\displaystyle=0.

Hence, as the coupling parameter
a\to 0
vanishes, we obtain the critical ideal Bose gas density, and as
a\to\infty
the critical density decreases indicating BEC for much lower particle densities. When the coupling parameter
a
increases the number of finite cycles is suppressed in the probability measure, and therefore the system undergoes a transition to a regime where the particle density is realised in so-called infinite cycles. The CMF model has not been studied in the literature so far, it shows similar behaviour as the ideal Bose gas because the Hamiltonian adds only weight on large numbers of cycles present. The following models involving the physical particle density have a more complex phase transition behaviour. As in the ideal Bose gas, the condensate density in Theorem
2.15
can be computed only in the sub-critical regime. This is again, due to the degenerate behaviour of the distribution.

2.2.3.
Thermodynamics of the PMF Bose Gas

We collect our findings for the PMF model. Our results are using rigorous large deviation analysis and all possible ranges of the chemical potential. We obtain all results in
[
BCMP05
]
with a completely different method for all values of the chemical potential, in addition, we compute the condensate density in Theorem
2.18
below. The density square term in the Hamiltonian stabilises the distribution such that the limiting distribution is no longer degenerate, allowing us the compute the partial derivatives for all values of the chemical potential. We identify regimes where the expected particle density equals the density of the rate function zero or not.

Proposition 2.16
.

For
\beta>0
, the pressure
p^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}}})}}\left(\beta,\cdot\right)\in C^{1}\left(\mathbb{R}\right)
and is convex. In particular,

\displaystyle\mu+\alpha<a\varrho_{\mathrm{c}}

\displaystyle\implies\frac{{\rm d}p^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}}})}}{{\rm d}\mu}=D\left(\xi^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}}})}\right)>\frac{\mu+\alpha}{a}

\displaystyle\mu+\alpha=a\varrho_{\mathrm{c}}

\displaystyle\implies\frac{{\rm d}p^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}}})}}{{\rm d}\mu}=D\left(\xi^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}}})}\right)=\frac{\mu+\alpha}{a}

\displaystyle\mu+\alpha>a\varrho_{\mathrm{c}}

\displaystyle\implies\frac{{\rm d}p^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}}})}}{{\rm d}\mu}=\frac{\mu+\alpha}{a}>D\left(\xi^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}}})}\right).

Proposition 2.17
.

Let
\alpha\leq 0
. For
\varrho>0
, the PMF Bose gas free energy is defined as the Legendre-Fenchel transform of the pressure,

f^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}}})}\left(\beta,\varrho\right):=\sup_{\mu\in\mathbb{R}}\left\{(\mu+\alpha)\varrho-p^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}}})}\left(\beta,\mu,\alpha\right)\right\}=f\left(\beta,\varrho\right)+\frac{a}{2}\varrho^{2}.

(2.18)

Theorem 2.18
.

For all
\beta>0
,
\mu\in\mathbb{R}
and
\alpha\leq 0
,

\displaystyle\Delta^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}}})}\left(\beta,\mu,\alpha\right)

\displaystyle=\Big(\frac{{\rm d}p^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}}})}}{{\rm d}\mu}\left(\beta,\mu,\alpha\right)-\varrho_{\mathrm{c}}\left(\beta\right)\Big)_{+}=\Big(\frac{\mu+\alpha}{a}-\varrho_{\mathrm{c}}\left(\beta\right)\Big)_{+}

\displaystyle=\Big(\frac{\partial}{\partial\mu}\left(H^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}}})}-H^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}}})}_{\mu+\alpha,l.s.c.}\Big)\right)\left(\xi^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}}})}\right),

where
\xi^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}}})}
is the unique minimiser (zero) of the rate function
I^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}}})}_{\mu,\alpha}
.

\mu+\alpha

, where it follows the dashed plot.

Conclusion.
The BEC phase transition is established in various equivalent ways, in Theorem
2.18
it is shown that the excess particle density is carried by so-called loops of unbounded length. Alternatively, Proposition
2.16
and Proposition
2.17
establish the phase transition via the change of the pressure density relation. The advantage of our LDP approach is that the rate function has unique zero and not an approximating sequence of minimiser. This is due to the fact that we are using the lower semicontinuous regularisation of the energy proving the large deviation principle. A close inspection of Figure
2

reveals this. For
d\geq 3
, we know that
a\varrho_{\rm c}<\infty
, and thus the density of the zero of the rate function is constant for all
\mu+\alpha\geq a\varrho_{\rm c}
. In this region, the total particle density is the dashed line intersecting the point
(a\varrho_{\rm c},\varrho_{\rm c})
. The so-called condensate density is then
(\frac{\mu+\alpha}{a}-\varrho_{\rm c})_{+}=\Delta^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}}})}(\beta,\mu,\alpha)
.

Thermodynamics of the HYL model

We collect our findings for the HYL model. Note that our results hold for all parameter
a>b
wheres the ones in
[

a=2b

[

.
We first identify the sub-critical regime where the expected physical particle density equals the density of the possible zeroes of the HYL rate function.

.

There exists
\tilde{\mu}=\tilde{\mu}\left(d,\beta,a,b\right)\in\mathbb{R}

for this range of
\mu
.
\xi^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{792963}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle HYL$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{792963}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle HYL$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{585927}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle HYL$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{585928}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle HYL$}}}}}}})}
is given in Proposition

.

.

.

For
\beta>0
,
\mu\in\mathbb{R}
,
\alpha\leq 0
, where the derivative is defined,

\displaystyle\Delta^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{792963}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle HYL$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{792963}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle HYL$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{585927}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle HYL$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{585928}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle HYL$}}}}}}})}\left(\beta,\mu,\alpha\right)

(2.19)

\displaystyle=-\lim_{K\to\infty}\left.\frac{{\rm d}}{{\rm d}s}\left(\inf_{\ell_{1}}\left\{\frac{1}{\beta}I^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{792963}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle HYL$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{792963}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle HYL$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{585927}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle HYL$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{585928}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle HYL$}}}}}}})}_{\mu+s,\alpha}+sD_{K}\right\}-p^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{792963}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle HYL$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{792963}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle HYL$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{585927}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle HYL$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{585928}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle HYL$}}}}}}})}\left(\beta,\mu+s,\alpha\right)\right)\right|_{s=0}.

for some

, then

\Delta^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{792963}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle HYL$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{792963}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle HYL$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{585927}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle HYL$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{585928}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle HYL$}}}}}}})}\left(\beta,\mu,\alpha\right)=\frac{a}{a-b}\left(\frac{\mu+\alpha}{a}-D\left(\xi\left(0\right)\right)\right)_{+}=\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial\mu}\left(H^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{792963}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle HYL$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{792963}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle HYL$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{585927}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle HYL$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{585928}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle HYL$}}}}}}})}_{\mu+\alpha}-H^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{792963}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle HYL$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{792963}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle HYL$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{585927}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle HYL$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{585928}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle HYL$}}}}}}})}_{\mu+\alpha,l.s.c.}\right)\right)\left(\xi\left(0\right)\right).

(2.20)

Conclusion.
The BEC phase transition for the HYL model is established as follows. Proposition
2.19
establishes a subcritical regime showing that for all
\mu+\alpha\leq\tilde{\mu}
the pressure is smooth and its derivative gives the particle density with no condensation.
In Proposition
2.20
we identify a regime for the inverse temperature where the pressure-density relation is broken. Depending on the density
D(\xi(0))
, for large enough
\mu
the particle density in loops of unbounded length is not vanishing. In Figure
3
we can identify the regime
\mu+\alpha\geq\bar{\mu}
when the density of the zero of the rate function is decreasing with
\mu
such that the excess density is carried by loops of unbounded length. If we choose
a=2b
in (
2.20
), we can recover the results in
[
Lew86
]
and
[
BLP88
]
.
It shows in fact, that for increasing values of the coupling parameter
a
, the condensate density decreases. On the other hand, if the parameter for the counter energy term,
b
, is approaching
a
, the condensate density increases. This is due to the fact that with large counter terms the system distributes the physical particles in as few as possible different cycles lengths. To accommodate the particle density, the only way is to put them in infinitely long cycles. Our analysis actually shows that the BEC phase transition for HYL is more complex and requires further detailed study, see
[
AD18b
]
.

2.3.
Relevance and discussion

One of the most prominent open problems in mathematical physics is the understanding of
Bose-Einstein condensation (BEC)
, a phase transition in an interacting many-particle system when a sufficiently low temperature is reached. That is, a macroscopic part of the system condenses to a state which is highly correlated and coherent. The first experimental realisation of BEC was only in 1995, and it has been awarded with a Nobel prize. In spite of an enormous research activity, this phase transition has withstood a mathematical proof yet. Only partial successes have been achieved, like the description of the free energy of the ideal, i.e., non-interacting, system (already contained in Bose’s and Einstein’s seminal paper in 1925) or the analysis of mean-field models (e.g.
[
To90
,
DMP05
,
AD08
]
) or the analysis of dilute systems at vanishing temperature
[
LSSY05
]
or the proof of BEC in lattice systems with half-filling
[
LSSY05
]
. However, the original problem for non-vanishing expected particle density and temperature is still waiting for a promising attack. The main purpose of the present paper is to provide representation formulae for the thermodynamic limit of the pressure in the so-called grand canonical ensemble and to show onset of BEC using these representations in various interaction models. We study first the well-known ideal Bose gas and its novel cousin the CMF model. The CMF model shows onset of BEC as the ideal Bose gas only for different critical values. We then analyse the well-known PMF model but we allow for strictly positive chemical potential. To prove the large deviation principle for the PMF model it is necessary to consider the lower semicontinuous regularisation of the mean-field energy term. This regularisation has not been done in the literature yet, see
[
BCMP05
]
, and it allows to find a minimum for the pressure representation for any set of parameters (inverse temperature, chemical potential and coupling constant), and thus establishes a rigorous and streamlined approach improving all previous studies of particle mean-field models which have to consider sequences of approximating minimisers.
Our interaction potentials take also negative values for the HYL model. Our analysis for the HYL model shows that the BEC is more complex with the negative counter term and that it depends on the interaction parameter
a
and
b
as well as the thermodynamic parameter.

The mathematical description of bosons is in terms of the symmetrised trace of the negative exponential of the corresponding Hamiltonian times the inverse temperature. The symmetrisation creates long range correlations of the interacting particles making the analysis an extremely challenging endeavour. The Feynman-Kac formula gives, in a natural way, a representation in terms of an expansion with respect to the cycles of random paths. It is conjectured by Feynman
[
Fe53
]
that BEC is signalled by the decisive appearance of a macroscopic amount of ‘infinite’ cycles, i.e. cycles whose lengths diverge with the number of particles. This phenomenon is also signalled by a loss of probability mass in the distribution of the ‘finite’ cycles. See
[
Sü02
]
for proofs of this coincidence in the ideal Bose gas and some mean-field models. A different line of research is studying the effect of the symmetrisation in random permutation and random partition models, see
[
Ver96
]
,
[
BCMP05
]
,
[
AD08
,
AK08
,
Ada
]
, or in spatial random permutation models going back to
[
F91
]
.

In the present paper, we prove large deviation principles for all our models, and as such generalise recent work in
[
ACK11
]
where upper and lower large deviation bounds do not match. In current work
[
AD18b
]
, we analyse the details of the HYL model and its BEC transition in more depth extending
[
BLP88
]
in a significant way. In future work, our aim is to establish level-3 large deviation principles for Lennard-Jones type potentials in the grand canonical ensemble for pressure representation formulae.

The methods used in the present paper are mainly probabilistic and are extensions and adaptations of recent work
[
ACK11
]
. Our starting point is the well-known Feynman-Kac formula, which translates the partition function in terms of an expectation over a large symmetrised system of interacting Brownian bridge paths. In a second step, which is also well-known, we reduce the combinatorial complexity by concatenating the bridges, using the symmetrisation. The novelty of the approach in
[
ACK11
]
is a reformulation of this system in terms of an expectation with respect to a
marked Poisson point process
, which serves as a reference process. This is a Poisson process in the space
\mathbb{R}^{d}
to whose particles we attach cycles called marks, starting and ending at that particle. The symmetrisation is reflected by an
a priori
distribution of cycle lengths.

Approaches to Bose gases using point processes have occasionally been used in the past (see
[
F91
]
and the references therein) and also in
[
Raf09
]
, but systems with interactions have not yet been considered using this technique, to the best of our knowledge.

The greatest advantage of this approach is that it is amenable to a large deviations analysis. The central objects here are the
empirical cycle count
and the
empirical particle density
of the marked point process. For some class of interacting systems, this direction of research was explored in
[
GZ93
,
G94
]
. In the present paper, we apply these ideas to the more difficult case of the interacting Bose gas.

3.
Proof of the Large deviations principles

This section contains the proofs for all large deviation principles. In Section
3.1
we establish the LDP for the ideal Bose gas model. We use this large deviation principle as a stepping stone towards arriving at LDPs for our interaction models. Section
3.2
gives the proof for the cycle mean-field (CMF) LDP, Section
3.3
for particle mean-field (PMF) LDP, and Section
3.4
the hard-core (HYL) LDP.

3.1.
Proof of Proposition
1.2
- Ideal Bose Gas LDP

Remark 3.1
.

The condition that
\mu\leq 0
arises from the
\overline{q}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mu}})}}
term. Our reference marked Poisson point process is superposition of independent marked Poisson point processes on
\mathbb{R}^{d}\times{\mathcal{C}}_{k}
with intensity measure given in (
1.7
). The superposition is itself a marked Poisson point process if and only if
\overline{q}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mu}})}}<\infty
. Clearly,
\overline{q}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mu}})}}
is finite if and only if
\mu\leq 0
.
\diamond

Our derivation of this LDP will be based on applying Baldi’s Theorem. We recall that theorem for the convenience of the reader in the following lemma.

Lemma 3.2

(
Baldi’s Theorem
)
.

Suppose
(\nu_{N})_{N\in\mathbb{N}}
is an exponentially tight sequence of measures on
\ell_{1}(\mathbb{R})
. Let
\Lambda\colon\ell_{\infty}(\mathbb{R})\to[0,\infty]
be the limiting cumulant generating function, and suppose that it exists and is finite for
t\in\ell_{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
. If
\Lambda
is Gâteaux differentiable, and lower semicontinuous on
\ell_{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
, then
(\nu_{N})_{N\in\mathbb{N}}
satisfies an LDP with rate function

\Lambda^{*}(x)=\sup_{t\in\ell_{\infty}(\mathbb{R})}\big\{\langle t,x\rangle-\Lambda(t)\big\},\qquad x\in\ell_{1}(\mathbb{R}).

(3.1)

We shall now set about establishing that the hypotheses of Baldi’s Theorem are satisfied. We adapt a beautiful proof in a recent study of Bosonic loop measures on graphs given in
[
Dan15
]
.

Lemma 3.3
.

For every
\mu\leq 0
,
\big(\nu_{N,\mu}\big)_{N\in\mathbb{N}}
is an exponentially tight sequence of measures.

Proof.
Suppose there exists an
x=x\left(\alpha\right)\in\ell_{1}\left(\mathbb{R}\right)
such that for all
k\geq 1
,

\limsup_{N\rightarrow\infty}\frac{1}{\left|\Lambda_{N}\right|}\log\nu_{N,\mu}\left(\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({k}})}}_{N}\geq x_{k}\right)<-2^{-k}\alpha,

where
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{N}=\big(\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({k}})}}_{N}\big)_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
is an
\ell_{1}\left(\mathbb{R}\right)
-valued random variable with law
\nu_{N,\mu}
. Also, define the set

K=\left\{y\in\ell_{1}\left(\mathbb{R}\right)\colon\left|y_{k}\right|\leq\left|x_{k}\right|\forall k\geq 1\right\},\qquad x\in\ell_{1}(\mathbb{R}).

To show compactness of
K
it is first easy to see that
K
is bounded and closed. Boundedness follows from
\lVert y\rVert_{\ell_{1}(\mathbb{R})}\leq\lVert x\rVert_{\ell_{1}(\mathbb{R})}
for all
y\in K
. Suppose that
K
is not closed, that is, there exists a sequence
y^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({n}})}}\in K
with limit
y^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({n}})}}\to y\notin K
as
n\to\infty
. Suppose that
\lvert y_{k}\rvert>\lvert x_{k}\rvert
for
y\notin K
and some
k\in\mathbb{N}
. Choose
\varepsilon=\frac{1}{2}(\lvert y_{k}\rvert-\lvert x_{k}\rvert)
, then, for
n
sufficiently large,

\lvert y_{k}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({n}})}}-y_{k}\rvert\leq\sum_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\lvert y^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({n}})}}_{j}-y_{j}\rvert<\frac{1}{2}(\lvert y_{k}\rvert-\lvert x_{k}\rvert),

which implies that

\lvert y^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({n}})}}_{k}\rvert>\lvert y_{k}\rvert-\varepsilon=\frac{1}{2}(\lvert y_{k}\rvert+\lvert x_{k}\rvert)>\lvert x_{k}\rvert,

contradicting
y^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({n}})}}\in K
. Hence,
K
is closed. It remains to show that
K
is totally bounded. From that, we shall find a finite cover of
\varepsilon
-open balls for
K
. Pick
\varepsilon>0
, and choose
N\in\mathbb{N}
such that
\sum_{k>N}\lvert x_{k}\rvert<\varepsilon/2
, and define the so-called cut-off sequences
\widetilde{K}=\{y\in K\colon y_{k}=0,k>N\}
. Clearly,
\widetilde{K}
is isomorphic to the totally bounded set

[-\lvert x_{1}\rvert,\lvert x_{1}\rvert]\times\cdots[-\lvert x_{N}\rvert,\lvert x_{N}\rvert]\subset\mathbb{R}^{N},

and thus it is itself totally bounded. There exist
w^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({1}})}},\ldots,w^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({M}})}}\in\widetilde{K}
such that

\widetilde{K}\subset\bigcup_{i=1}^{M}B(w^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({i}})}},\frac{\varepsilon}{2}).

For any
y\in
denote
\widetilde{y}\in\widetilde{K}
the sequences which agrees with
y
on the first
N
terms, and choose
w^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({i}})}}
such that
\widetilde{y}\in B(w^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({i}})}},\frac{\varepsilon}{2})
. Then,

\lVert y-w^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({i}})}}\rVert_{\ell_{1}(\mathbb{R})}=\sum_{k=1}^{N}\lvert\widetilde{y}_{k}-w^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({i}})}}_{k}\rvert+\sum_{k>N}\lvert y_{k}\rvert<\frac{\varepsilon}{2}+\frac{\varepsilon}{2}.

Thus,
K\subset\bigcup_{i=1}^{M}B(w^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({i}})}},\frac{\varepsilon}{2})
, and we conclude with the compactness of
K
.

Now, since the
\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({k}})}}_{N}
are independent, we have

\limsup_{N\rightarrow\infty}\frac{1}{\left|\Lambda_{N}\right|}\log\nu_{N,\mu}\left(K^{\rm c}\right)=\limsup_{N\rightarrow\infty}\frac{1}{\left|\Lambda_{N}\right|}\sum_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\log\nu_{N,\mu}\left(\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({k}})}}_{N}>x_{k}\right)<-\alpha,

and conclude with the statement in the lemma.
All that remains now is to find such a sequence
x
. We consider each
x_{k}
in turn. For all constants
c\geq 0
, and
\tau>0
, we have the Chernoff bound

\displaystyle\nu_{N,\mu}\left(\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({k}})}}_{N}>c\right)

\displaystyle=\nu_{N,\mu}\left({\rm e}^{\frac{\tau}{\lvert\Lambda_{N}\rvert}{\mathcal{N}}_{k}}>{\rm e}^{\tau c}\right)

\displaystyle\leq{\rm e}^{-\tau c}{\tt E}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({k}})}}\left[{\rm e}^{\frac{\tau}{\lvert\Lambda_{N}\rvert}{\mathcal{N}}_{k}}\right]

\displaystyle={\rm e}^{-\tau c}\exp\left(\left|\Lambda_{N}\right|q^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mu}})}}_{k}\left({\rm e}^{\frac{\tau}{\left|\Lambda_{N}\right|}}-1\right)\right).

The inequality is an application of Markov’s inequality, and the expectation is nothing other than the moment generating function of
{\mathcal{N}}_{k}
and
{\tt E}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({k}})}}
is the expectation with respect to the Poisson point process on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
with intensity
q^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mu}})}}_{k}
. Since
{\mathcal{N}}_{k}
is a Poisson random variable with mean
\lvert\Lambda_{N}\rvert q^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mu}})}}_{k}
, this can be calculated.

Differentiating this bound with respect to
\tau
gives us that the minimum occurs at
\tau^{*}=\lvert\Lambda_{N}\rvert\log\frac{c}{q^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mu}})}}_{k}}
. If
c>0
, then
\tau^{*}>0
for sufficiently large
N
. This means that we can optimise this form of bound as

\nu_{N,\mu}(\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({k}})}}_{N}>c)\leq\Big(\frac{c}{q^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mu}})}}_{k}}\Big)^{-\left|\Lambda_{N}\right|c}\exp\left(\left|\Lambda_{N}\right|\left(c-q^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mu}})}}_{k}\right)\right).

Taking
N\rightarrow\infty
then gives us

\limsup_{N\rightarrow\infty}\frac{1}{\lvert\Lambda_{N}\rvert}\log\nu_{N,\mu}(\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({k}})}}_{N}>c)\leq c-q^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mu}})}}_{k}-c\log\frac{c}{q^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mu}})}}_{k}}.

Now note that on
c>0
, the maps

c\mapsto c-q^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mu}})}}_{k}-c\log\frac{c}{q^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mu}})}}_{k}}+2^{-k}\alpha,\qquad k\in\mathbb{N},

are differentiable, strictly decreasing, and have at most a unique zero
c^{*}_{k}
. If there does not exist such a zero, then the map is strictly negative, and it will suffice in what follows to set
c^{*}_{k}=0
. Since our maps are strictly negative for
c>c^{*}_{k}
, we only need to find a sequence
x
such that
x_{k}>c^{*}_{k}
for all
k
. Now we only need to find such an
x\in\ell_{1}\left(\mathbb{R}\right)
.

Consider
x_{k}=c^{*}_{k}+2^{-k}
. Therefore
x\in\ell_{1}\left(\mathbb{R}\right)
if and only if
c^{*}\in\ell_{1}\left(\mathbb{R}\right)
. If we defined
c^{*}_{k}
as a zero, then
c^{*}_{k}
solves

c^{*}_{k}\Big(1-\log\Big(\frac{c^{*}_{k}}{q^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mu}})}}_{k}}\Big)\Big)=q^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mu}})}}_{k}+2^{-k}\alpha.

Otherwise,
c^{*}_{k}=0
and

c^{*}_{k}\Big(1-\log\Big(\frac{c^{*}_{k}}{q^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mu}})}}_{k}}\Big)\Big)=0.

So noting that
q^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mu}})}}_{k},\alpha>0
and that the sum of
q^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mu}})}}_{k}
converges give us

\sum_{k\in\mathbb{N}}c^{*}_{k}\Big(1-\log\Big(\frac{c^{*}_{k}}{q^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mu}})}}_{k}}\Big)\Big)\leq\alpha+\sum_{k\in\mathbb{N}}q^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mu}})}}_{k}<\infty.

Suppose, for contradiction, that
\sum_{k\in\mathbb{N}}c^{*}_{k}=\infty
. Then, in order for the left hand side of the above inequality to converge, we require
1-\log\Big(\frac{c^{*}_{k}}{q^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mu}})}}_{k}}\Big)\rightarrow 0
as
k\rightarrow\infty
. Consequently, there exists a
K\geq 1
such that for
k\geq K
,
\frac{c^{*}_{k}}{q^{\star}_{k}}<3
, and hence

\sum_{k\geq K}c^{*}_{k}\leq 3\sum_{k\geq K}q^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mu}})}}_{k}\leq 3\sum_{k\in\mathbb{N}}q^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mu}})}}_{k}<\infty.

We have a contradiction, and
\sum_{k\in\mathbb{N}}c^{*}_{k}<\infty
as required.∎

Recall that
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{N}
is a
\ell_{1}\left(\mathbb{R}\right)
-valued random variable with law
\nu_{N,\mu}
, and let
t\in\ell_{\infty}\left(\mathbb{R}\right)
, the dual space. Then, the
limit cumulant generating function
is given by

\Lambda(t)=\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}\frac{1}{\lvert\Lambda_{N}\rvert}\log\mathbb{E}_{\nu_{N,\mu}}\Big[\exp\Big(\lvert\Lambda_{N}\rvert\langle t,\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{N}\rangle\Big)\Big],\qquad t\in\ell_{\infty}(\mathbb{R}).

(3.2)

Lemma 3.4
.

The limit cumulant generating function exists and is given by

\Lambda\left(t\right)=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{N}}q^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mu}})}}_{k}\left({\rm e}^{t_{k}}-1\right)<\infty,\qquad t\in\ell_{\infty}\left(\mathbb{R}\right).

Moreover,
\Lambda
is Gâteaux differentiable, lower semicontinuous, and strictly convex.

Proof.

First, let us evaluate the logarithmic moment generating function. Recall, that our reference process is a independent superposition of countably many independent marked Poisson point processes. Denote the marginal law of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({k}})}}_{N}
by
\nu^{(k)}_{N}
, then we have,

\displaystyle\Lambda\left(t\right)

\displaystyle=\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}\frac{1}{\lvert\Lambda_{N}\rvert}\log\mathbb{E}_{\nu_{N,\mu}}\Big[\exp\Big(\lvert\Lambda_{N}\rvert\langle t,\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{N}\rangle\Big)\Big]=\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}\frac{1}{\left|\Lambda_{N}\right|}\sum_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\log\mathbb{E}_{\nu^{(k)}_{N}}\left[\exp\left(\lvert\Lambda_{N}\rvert t_{k}\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({k}})}}_{N}\right)\right]

\displaystyle=\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}\frac{1}{\left|\Lambda_{N}\right|}\sum_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\left|\Lambda_{N}\right|q^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mu}})}}_{k}\left({\rm e}^{t_{k}}-1\right)=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{N}}q^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mu}})}}_{k}\left({\rm e}^{t_{k}}-1\right).

Here, we were able to evaluate the expectation with respect to
\nu^{(k)}_{N}
using the logarithmic moment generating function for a Poisson distribution and recalling that
\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({k}})}}_{N}
has mean
q^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mu}})}}_{k}
.

To see that
\Lambda\left(t\right)
is finite, note that
t\in\ell_{\infty}\left(\mathbb{R}\right)
implies that
T:=\sup_{j\in\mathbb{N}}t_{j}
is finite. Hence

\Lambda\left(t\right)\leq\left({\rm e}^{T}-1\right)\overline{q}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mu}})}}<\infty.

To confirm Gâteaux differentiability, let
t,s\in\ell_{\infty}\left(\mathbb{R}\right)
and consider

\frac{{\rm d}}{{\rm d}\epsilon}\Lambda\left(t+\epsilon s\right)=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{N}}q^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mu}})}}_{k}s_{k}{\rm e}^{t_{k}+\epsilon s_{k}}.

This sum is finite because
t
and
s
are bounded above and
q^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mu}})}}\in\ell_{1}\left(\mathbb{R}\right)
for
\mu<0
. In particular, the derivative is defined at
\epsilon=0
, and hence
\Lambda
is Gâteaux differentiable.

Lower semicontinuity is an immediate consequence of Fatou’s Lemma. For any sequence
t^{(n)}\rightarrow t
in
\ell_{\infty}\left(\mathbb{R}\right)
,

\liminf_{n\rightarrow\infty}\Lambda\left(t^{(n)}\right)=\liminf_{n\rightarrow\infty}\sum_{k\in\mathbb{N}}q^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mu}})}}_{k}\left({\rm e}^{t^{(n)}_{k}}-1\right)\geq\sum_{k\in\mathbb{N}}q^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mu}})}}_{k}\left({\rm e}^{t_{k}}-1\right)=\Lambda\left(t\right).

To show strict convexity, consider distinct
t,s\in\ell_{\infty}\left(\mathbb{R}\right)
and
\lambda\in\left[0,1\right]
. Then

\displaystyle\Lambda\left(\lambda s+\left(1-\lambda\right)t\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{N}}q^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mu}})}}_{k}\left({\rm e}^{\lambda s_{k}+\left(1-\lambda\right)t_{k}}-1\right)

\displaystyle<\lambda\sum_{k\in\mathbb{N}}q^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mu}})}}_{k}{\rm e}^{s_{k}}+\left(1-\lambda\right)\sum_{k\in\mathbb{N}}q^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mu}})}}_{k}{\rm e}^{t_{k}}-\overline{q}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mu}})}}

\displaystyle=\lambda\Lambda\left(s\right)+\left(1-\lambda\right)\Lambda\left(t\right),

where the inequality follows from the strict convexity of the exponential function.
∎

Remark 3.5
.

If we do not have
\mu\leq 0
, then we do not have
\Lambda\left(t\right)<\infty
for all
t\in\ell_{\infty}\left(\mathbb{R}\right)
. To see this, let
t
be a constant sequence
t_{k}=C>0
. Then
\Lambda\left(t\right)=C\overline{q}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mu}})}}=\infty
unless
\mu\leq 0
.
\diamond

Lemma 3.6
.

For all
x\in\ell_{1}\left(\mathbb{R}\right)
, we have

\Lambda^{*}\left(x\right):=\sup_{t\in\ell_{\infty}\left(\mathbb{R}\right)}\left\{\left\langle t,x\right\rangle-\Lambda\left(t\right)\right\}=I(x).

Proof.

Let
g_{x}\left(t\right)
denote the functional we wish to maximise in the definition of
\Lambda^{*}
, so

g_{x}\left(t\right)=\sum^{\infty}_{k=1}\left[x_{k}t_{k}+q^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mu}})}}_{k}\left(1-{\rm e}^{t_{k}}\right)\right].

First let us consider
x\in\ell_{1}\left(\mathbb{R}\right)\setminus\ell_{1}\left(\mathbb{R}_{+}\right)
. Hence there exists an index
k^{\prime}
such that
x_{k^{\prime}}<0
. Now let
t^{(T)}=-T\delta_{k^{\prime}}\in\ell_{\infty}\left(\mathbb{R}\right)
. Therefore

\Lambda^{*}\left(x\right)\geq g_{x}\left(t^{(T)}\right)=-Tx_{k^{\prime}}+q^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mu}})}}_{k^{\prime}}\left(1-{\rm e}^{-T}\right)\xrightarrow{T\rightarrow\infty}+\infty.

This means
\Lambda^{*}\left(x\right)=+\infty=I\left(x\right)
for all
x\in\ell_{1}\left(\mathbb{R}\right)\setminus\ell_{1}\left(\mathbb{R}_{+}\right)
.

To show the required inequality on
\ell_{1}\left(\mathbb{R}\right)
, let us now search for critical points of
g_{x}
. We know
g_{x}
is Gâteaux differentiable because it is the sum of a linear term (with coefficients
x\in\ell_{1}\left(\mathbb{R}\right)
) and a Gâteaux differentiable term. Taking the Gâteaux derivative of
g_{x}
gives us

{\rm d}g_{x}\left(t;s\right)=\sum^{\infty}_{k=1}s_{k}\left(x_{k}-q^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mu}})}}_{k}{\rm e}^{t_{k}}\right),\qquad\forall t,s\in\ell_{\infty}\left(\mathbb{R}\right).

Now
t
is a critical point if and only if
{\rm d}g_{x}\left(t;s\right)=0

\forall s\in\ell_{\infty}\left(\mathbb{R}\right)
. This means that we want to investigate the sequence
\tilde{t}_{k}=\log\frac{x_{k}}{q^{\mu}_{k}}
. If
\tilde{t}\in\ell_{\infty}\left(\mathbb{R}\right)
, then this gives us the supremum, and a simple substitution tells us that
\Lambda^{*}\left(x\right)=I\left(x\right)
for such
x
. Unfortunately, this is not necessarily the case.

Nevertheless, these critical points will give us the supremum over all sequences in
\left(\mathbb{R}\cup\left\{-\infty\right\}\right)^{\mathbb{N}}
. Since
\ell_{\infty}\left(\mathbb{R}\right)\subset\left(\mathbb{R}\cup\left\{-\infty\right\}\right)^{\mathbb{N}}
, we have

\Lambda^{*}\left(x\right)=\sup_{t\in\ell_{\infty}\left(\mathbb{R}\right)}g_{x}\left(t\right)\leq\sup_{t\in\left(\mathbb{R}\cup\left\{-\infty\right\}\right)^{\mathbb{N}}}g_{x}\left(t\right)=I\left(x\right).

(3.3)

To find the reverse inequality, let us consider

t^{(K)}_{k}=\begin{cases}\mathds{1}\left\{k\leq K\right\}\log\frac{x_{k}}{q^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mu}})}}_{k}}&,x_{k}\neq 0,\\
-K\mathds{1}\left\{k\leq K\right\}&,x_{k}=0.\end{cases}

Since
t^{(K)}
truncates, it is clearly in
\ell_{\infty}\left(\mathbb{R}\right)
for all
K
. Now let us substitute it into
g_{x}
.

\displaystyle g_{x}\left(t^{(K)}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k\leq K:x_{k}\neq 0}\left(x_{k}\log\frac{x_{k}}{q^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mu}})}}_{k}}-x_{k}+q^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mu}})}}_{k}\right)+\sum_{k\leq K:x_{k}=0}q^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mu}})}}_{k}\left(1-{\rm e}^{-K}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum^{K}_{k=1}\left[x_{k}\left(\log\frac{x_{k}}{q^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mu}})}}_{k}}-1\right)+q^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mu}})}}_{k}\right]-{\rm e}^{-K}\sum_{k\leq K\colon x_{k}=0}q^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mu}})}}_{k}

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{K\rightarrow\infty}I\left(x\right).

In the second equality, we have used the convention that
0\log 0=0
. The limit holds because the sum of the
q^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mu}})}}_{k}
converges, and the sum defining
I\left(x\right)
converges.

This sequence
\left(t^{(K)}\right)_{K\in\mathbb{N}}
shows that for
x\in\ell_{1}\left(\mathbb{R}_{+}\right)
,

\Lambda^{*}\left(x\right)=\sup_{t\in\ell_{\infty}\left(\mathbb{R}\right)}g_{x}\left(t\right)\geq I\left(x\right),

as required.
∎

Using Baldi’s Theorem in Lemma
3.2
in conjunction with Lemma
3.3
,
3.4
,
3.6
, we conclude with the statement in Proposition
1.2
.∎

3.2.
Proof of Theorem
1.3
- Cycle Mean Field LDP

Recall that
\mathcal{N}_{k}
is a Poisson random variable describing the number of cycles in our loop soup with length
\beta k
, and that the total cycle number is the random variable
N_{\Lambda_{N}}=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{N}}{\mathcal{N}}_{k}
.
We are going to apply Varadahn’s Lemma in
[
DZ09
, Theorem 4.3.1]
. To show continuity of
H^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{855497}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle CMF$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{855497}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle CMF$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{647441}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle CMF$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{647440}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle CMF$}}}}}}})}}
, let us show sequential continuity. This implies continuity since
\ell_{1}(\mathbb{R})
is a metric space. Let
x^{(n)}\rightarrow x
be a convergent sequence in
\ell_{1}(\mathbb{R})
, so
\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\sum_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\lvert x^{(n)}_{k}-x_{k}\rvert=0
.
Let
S(x):=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{N}}x_{k}
. Then

\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\lvert S(x^{(n)})-S(x)\rvert=\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\big|\sum_{k\in\mathbb{N}}x^{(n)}_{k}-\sum_{k\in\mathbb{N}}x_{k}\big|\leq\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\sum_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\lvert x^{(n)}_{k}-x_{k}\rvert=0.

Hence
S
is continuous. We can then write the Hamiltonian as the composition of continuous functions
H^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{855497}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle CMF$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{855497}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle CMF$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{647441}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle CMF$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{647440}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle CMF$}}}}}}})}}=T\circ S
, where
T\colon\mathbb{R}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
,
x\rightarrow\frac{a}{2}x^{2}
.
We can now simply apply Varadhan’s Lemma. The lower bound

\liminf_{N\to\infty}\frac{1}{\lvert\Lambda_{N}\rvert}\log\mathbb{E}_{\nu_{N,\alpha}}\Big[{\rm e}^{-\beta\lvert\Lambda_{N}\rvert H^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{855497}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle CMF$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{855497}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle CMF$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{647441}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle CMF$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{647440}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle CMF$}}}}}}})}}\Big]\geq\sup_{x\in\ell_{1}(\mathbb{R})}\big\{-\beta H^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{855497}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle CMF$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{855497}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle CMF$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{647441}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle CMF$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{647440}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle CMF$}}}}}}})}(x)-I_{\alpha}(x)\big\}

(3.4)

follows easily with
[
DZ09
, Lemma 4.3.4]
. For the corresponding upper bound we simply note that the tail-condition in
[
DZ09
, Theorem 4.3.1]
holds due to
H^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{855497}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle CMF$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{855497}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle CMF$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{647441}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle CMF$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{647440}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle CMF$}}}}}}})}(x)\geq 0
for all
x\in\ell_{1}(\mathbb{R}_{+})
. Therefore, with
[
DZ09
, Lemma 4.3.6]
we obtain the corresponding upper bound

\limsup_{N\to\infty}\frac{1}{\lvert\Lambda_{N}\rvert}\log\mathbb{E}_{\nu_{N,\alpha}}\Big[{\rm e}^{-\beta\lvert\Lambda_{N}\rvert H^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{855497}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle CMF$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{855497}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle CMF$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{647441}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle CMF$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{647440}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle CMF$}}}}}}})}}\Big]\leq\sup_{x\in\ell_{1}(\mathbb{R})}\big\{-\beta H^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{855497}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle CMF$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{855497}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle CMF$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{647441}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle CMF$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{647440}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle CMF$}}}}}}})}(x)-I_{\alpha}(x)\big\}.

(3.5)

We conclude with the statement in Theorem
1.3
by combining the lower bound (
3.4
) and the upper bound (
3.5
).∎

3.3.
Proof of Theorem
1.4
- Particle Mean Field LDP

This subsection gives the proof for Theorem
1.4
. To prove the large deviation principle for
\nu_{N,\mu,\alpha}^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}}})}
one would simply use Varadhan’s Lemma. However, the first term of the Hamiltonian
H^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}}})}_{\mu}
for
\mu>0
is not lower semicontinuous whereas the second term is only lower semicontinuous. Using
[
GZ93
]
, one would arrive at lower and upper bounds for
\mathbb{E}_{\nu_{N,\alpha}}[{\rm e}^{-\lvert\Lambda_{N}\rvert\beta H^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}}})}_{\mu}}]
using the upper and the lower semicontinuous regularisation of
H^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}}})}_{\mu}
, respectively. Unfortunately, the upper semicontinuous regularisation of the Hamiltonian equals infinity, and thus it does not provide a lower bound for the large deviation principle. Our strategy is therefore twofold. For the large deviation upper bound we use the lower semicontinuous regularisation in conjunction with the corresponding bound in Varadhan’s Lemma. We obtain the corresponding large deviation lower bound by conditioning that the empirical cycle count is supported on a finite-dimensional subspace. On this event we can replace our measure by the corresponding measure with finite dimensional mark space. On this subspace the Hamiltonian is in fact continuous and thus application of Varadhan’s Lemma provides a lower bound. To remove the cutoff parameter we will construct finite-dimensional sequences approximating the infimum of the corresponding lower bound.
We start with a couple of observations.

Lemma 3.7
.

For all
\mu>0
, the lower semicontinuous regularisation of
H^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}}})}_{\mu}
is given as

H^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}}})}}_{\mu,l.s.c.}(x)=H^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}}})}_{\mu}(x)-\frac{1}{2a}\big(\mu-aD(x)\big)_{+}^{2}=\begin{cases}-\mu D(x)+\frac{a}{2}D(x)^{2}&,D(x)\geq\frac{\mu}{a},\\
-\frac{\mu^{2}}{2a}&,D(x)<\frac{\mu}{a},\end{cases}\quad x\in\ell_{1}(\mathbb{R}_{+}),

(3.6)

whereas for all
\mu\leq 0
,
H^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}}})}_{\mu,l.s.c.}\equiv H^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}}})}_{\mu}
.

D
H^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}}})}}_{\mu,l.s.c.}
-\frac{\mu^{2}}{2a}
\frac{\mu}{a}

Figure 4
.
Sketch of
H^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646135}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646135}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646135}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646135}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}}})}_{\mu,l.s.c.}
as a function of the total particle density
D
.

Proof.
Clearly,
H^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}}})}_{\mu,l.s.c.}(x)\leq H^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}}})}_{\mu}(x)
for all
x\in\ell_{1}(\mathbb{R}_{+})
. Suppose that
\mu\leq 0
. Then the Hamiltonian
H^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}}})}_{\mu}
is lower semicontinuous and
