. Convexity follows from properties of the Bose functions,
g(1+\frac{d}{2},-\beta\mu)
, see (
B.1
) in Appendix
B
. (2) This follows from the Bose functions,
g(n,x)
, being differentiable for
x>0
, and

\frac{{\rm d}}{{\rm d}x}g(n,x)=-g(n-1,x),\qquad\forall x>0.

Then the first derivative follows from directly differentiating the representation (
2.7
).
∎

Proof of Proposition
2.10
.
(1) This follows by direct computation. The exponential term ensures that the derivative of the finite-volume pressure is increasing in
\mu
. As long as the box
\Lambda_{N}
has finite volume one can give the average particle density any pre-assigned value by choosing a chemical potential.

(2) The limit in (
2.12
) is obtained by direct calculation in conjunction with basic properties of the Bose function summarised in Appendix
B
. The convergence of the unique root is ensured as long as the expected particle density stays below the critical density which is finite only in dimensions
d\geq 3
.
∎

Proof of Proposition
2.11
.
Since
p\left(\beta,\mu\right)=+\infty
for
\mu>0
, we only need to search
\mu\leq 0
. On the interior of this region
p
is differentiable, and we look for stationary points. If
\varrho\geq\varrho_{\mathrm{c}}
, then there are no stationary points for
\mu<0
and
\mu\varrho-p\left(\beta,\mu\right)
is increasing in
\mu
. Hence the supremum is achieved at
\mu=0
. If
\varrho<\varrho_{\mathrm{c}}
, then there is a unique stationary point. This is also a local maximum and is given at
\mu=\alpha
as required. This has the required limit as
\varrho\uparrow\varrho_{\mathrm{c}}
implying the continuity for
f
. ∎

Proof of Theorem
2.12
.
Let us begin with the
\mu<0
case, because a similar approach will be important for our approaches to the PMF and HYL cases. For
s\leq-\mu
, fixed
N
, and fixed
K
, define

g^{\scriptscriptstyle{({K}})}_{N}\left(s\right):=\frac{1}{\beta\left|\Lambda_{N}\right|}\log\mathbb{E}_{\nu_{N,\mu}}\left[\exp\left(\left|\Lambda_{N}\right|s\beta\left(D-D_{K}\right)\right)\right].

Then

\left.\frac{{\rm d}g^{\scriptscriptstyle{({K}})}_{N}}{{\rm d}s}\right|_{s=0}=\mathbb{E}_{\nu_{N,\mu}}\left[D-D_{K}\right]\quad\text{and}\quad\Delta\left(\beta,\mu\right)=\lim_{K\to\infty}\lim_{N\to\infty}\left.\frac{{\rm d}g^{\scriptscriptstyle{({K}})}_{N}}{{\rm d}s}\right|_{s=0}.

Since
g^{\scriptscriptstyle{({K}})}_{N}
are all convex in
s
, we will use Griffith’s Lemma to get the point-wise limit of the derivative from the derivative of the point-wise limit:

\Delta\left(\beta,\mu\right)=\lim_{K\to\infty}\frac{{\rm d}}{{\rm d}s}\Big(\lim_{N\to\infty}g^{\scriptscriptstyle{({K}})}_{N}\left(s\right)\Big)\Big|_{s=0}.

To calculate the point-wise limit of
g^{\scriptscriptstyle{({K}})}_{N}
, we first rewrite
g^{\scriptscriptstyle{({K}})}_{N}
as

g^{\scriptscriptstyle{({K}})}_{N}\left(s\right)=\frac{1}{\beta\left|\Lambda_{N}\right|}\log\mathbb{E}_{\nu_{N,\mu+s}}\left[\exp\left(-\left|\Lambda_{N}\right|s\beta D_{K}\right)\right]+\frac{1}{\beta\left|\Lambda_{N}\right|}\log\frac{Z_{N}\left(\beta,\mu+s\right)}{Z_{N}\left(\beta,\mu\right)}.

Then we want to use Varadhan’s Lemma with our LDP for the ideal Bose gas measure and the tilt
\phi=-s\beta D_{K}
. This
\phi
is continuous, but we need to pay attention to the boundedness conditions. We will show

\lim_{M\to\infty}\limsup_{N\to\infty}\frac{1}{\left|\Lambda_{N}\right|}\mathbb{E}_{\nu_{N,\mu+s}}\left[{\rm e}^{\left|\Lambda_{N}\right|\phi}\mathds{1}\left\{\phi\geq M\right\}\right]=-\infty.

(4.9)

For
0\leq s\leq-\mu
, we have
\phi\leq 0
almost surely, so (
4.9
) holds trivially. For
s<0
we have to work a little harder. Since
\phi
is continuous, the set
\left\{\phi=m\right\}
is closed (and measurable). Hence our LDP for the ideal Bose gas model gives us a bound on the probability of this set:

\limsup_{N\rightarrow\infty}\frac{1}{\left|\Lambda_{N}\right|}\log\nu_{N,\mu+s}\left(\phi=m\right)\leq-\inf_{\phi=m}I_{\mu+s}\leq\bar{q}^{\scriptscriptstyle{({0}})}+\beta\left(\mu+s\right)\Big(\frac{m}{\left|s\right|\beta}\Big).

This means that for sufficiently large
N
there exists a
m
and
N
independent constant
C>\bar{q}^{\scriptscriptstyle{({0}})}
, such that

{\rm e}^{m\left|\Lambda_{N}\right|}\nu_{N,\mu+s}\left(\phi=m\right)\leq\exp\Big(\lvert\Lambda_{N}\rvert\big[C+\frac{\mu}{\lvert s\rvert}m\big]\Big).

Since
\mu<0
, we have sufficiently fast decay in
m
to prove that (
4.9
) holds even for
s<0
, and Varadhan gives us

\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}g^{\scriptscriptstyle{({K}})}_{N}\left(s\right)=-\inf_{\ell_{1}}\big\{\frac{1}{\beta}I_{\mu+s}+sD_{J}\big\}+p\left(\beta,\mu+s\right)-p\left(\beta,\mu\right),\qquad\forall s\leq-\mu.

In the style of Lemma
2.1
, we can find that this infimum is achieved at
\xi\left(s\right)\in\ell_{1}\left(\mathbb{R}_{+}\right)
, where

\xi_{k}=\begin{cases}q_{k}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mu}})}}&,k\leq K,\\
q_{k}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mu+s}})}}&,k>K.\end{cases}

Hence

\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}g^{\scriptscriptstyle{({K}})}_{N}\left(s\right)=\frac{1}{\beta}\sum^{\infty}_{j=K+1}\big(q^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mu+s}})}_{j}-q^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mu}})}_{j}\big),\qquad\frac{{\rm d}}{{\rm d}s}\Big(\lim_{N\to\infty}g^{\scriptscriptstyle{({K}})}_{N}\left(s\right)\Big)\Big|_{s=0}=\sum^{\infty}_{j=K+1}jq^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mu}})}_{j}.

Finally the sum vanishes as
K\to\infty
.

For
\mu=0
with
d=1,2
, we take a more direct approach. It is clear from our construction of the ideal Bose gas model that the point-wise limit
\lim_{N\to\infty}\mathbb{E}_{\nu_{N,\mu}}\left[jx_{j}\right]=jq^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mu}})}_{j}
. Then for all
M\in\mathbb{N}
,

\displaystyle\liminf_{N\to\infty}\mathbb{E}_{\nu_{N,\alpha}}\Big[\sum^{\infty}_{j=K+1}jx_{j}\Big]\geq\lim_{N\to\infty}\mathbb{E}_{\nu_{N,\mu}}\Big[\sum^{M}_{j=K+1}jx_{j}\Big]=\sum^{M}_{j=K+1}jq^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mu}})}_{j}.

Since this lower bound diverges as
M\to\infty
if
\mu=0
and
d=1,2
, we have our result for this case.

For
\mu=0
and
d\geq 3
, the behaviour changes depending upon the boundary condition. Nevertheless, by applying direct methods similar to the
\mu>0
case we get the required results.
∎

The CMF model

Proof of Proposition
2.13
.
(1) The continuity of
p^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{855497}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle CMF$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{855497}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle CMF$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{647441}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle CMF$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{647440}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle CMF$}}}}}}})}
for
\mu\leq 0
follows from (
2.8
) and the continuity of
K=a\beta\bar{q}^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mu}})}
and
W_{0}
. Convexity follows from considering the derivatives of
W_{0}\left(K\right)
with respect to
\mu
for
\mu<0
. Appendix
C
is useful for this calculation.

(2) Smoothness follows from
W_{0}
and the Bose functions being differentiable on the appropriate regions. The form of the first derivative can be found by either directly differentiating (
2.8
), or by using Lemma
4.1
with the zero found in Proposition
2.2
.

(3) We obtain (
2.16
) from (
2.12
) and the continuity of
W_{0}
and
\overline{q}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mu}})}}
.
∎

Proof of Proposition
2.14
.
This is proven in the same way as Proposition
2.11
.
∎

Proof of Theorem
2.15
.
This proof begins identically to Theorem
2.12
. For
s\leq-\mu
, fixed
N
, and fixed
K
, define

g^{\scriptscriptstyle{({K}})}_{N}\left(s\right):=\frac{1}{\beta\left|\Lambda_{N}\right|}\log\mathbb{E}_{\nu_{N,\mu}^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{855497}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle CMF$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{855497}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle CMF$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{647441}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle CMF$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{647440}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle CMF$}}}}}}})}}\left[\exp\left(\left|\Lambda_{N}\right|s\beta\left(D-D_{K}\right)\right)\right].

Then by rearranging terms and applying Varadhan, we find that for
s\leq-\mu
,

\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}g^{\scriptscriptstyle{({K}})}_{N}\left(s\right)=-\inf_{\ell_{1}}\big\{\frac{1}{\beta}I^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{855497}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle CMF$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{855497}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle CMF$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{647441}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle CMF$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{647440}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle CMF$}}}}}}})}_{\mu+s}+sD_{J}\big\}+p^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{855497}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle CMF$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{855497}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle CMF$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{647441}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle CMF$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{647440}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle CMF$}}}}}}})}\left(\beta,\mu+s\right)-p^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{855497}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle CMF$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{855497}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle CMF$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{647441}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle CMF$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{647440}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle CMF$}}}}}}})}\left(\beta,\mu\right),\qquad\forall s\leq-\mu.

In the style of Lemma
2.2
, we can find that this infimum is achieved at
\xi\left(s\right)\in\ell_{1}\left(\mathbb{R}_{+}\right)
, where

\xi_{k}=\begin{cases}\frac{W_{0}\left(a\beta\bar{q}^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mu}})}\right)}{a\beta\bar{q}^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mu}})}}q_{k}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mu}})}}&,k\leq K,\\
\frac{W_{0}\left(a\beta\bar{q}^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mu+s}})}\right)}{a\beta\bar{q}^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mu+s}})}}q_{k}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mu+s}})}}&,k>K.\end{cases}

Substituting this into the infimum, and then taking the derivative gives us

\frac{{\rm d}}{{\rm d}s}\Big(\lim_{N\to\infty}g^{\scriptscriptstyle{({K}})}_{N}\left(s\right)\Big)\Big|_{s=0}=\frac{W_{0}\left(a\beta\bar{q}^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mu}})}\right)}{a\beta\bar{q}^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mu}})}}\sum^{\infty}_{j=K+1}jq^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mu}})}_{j}.

Finally the sum vanishes as
K\to\infty
, and Griffith’s Lemma gives us the result.
∎

The PMF model

Proof of Proposition
2.16
.
This follows from either directly differentiating (
2.9
), or by using Lemma
4.1
with the zero found in Proposition
2.4
. Convexity also follows from Proposition
2.4
, noting that
D\left(\xi^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}}})}\right)
is continuous and increasing in
\mu
.
∎

Proof Proposition
2.17
.
The proof is obtained directly from our analysis above, or by application of (
1.30
) in Remark
1.10
.
∎

Proof of Theorem
2.18
.
Let us set
\alpha=0
and omit it from the notation. Our proof begins similarly to Theorems
2.12
and
2.15
. For
\mu,s\in\mathbb{R}
, fixed
N
, and fixed
K
, define

g^{\scriptscriptstyle{({K}})}_{N}\left(s\right)=\frac{1}{\beta\left|\Lambda_{N}\right|}\log\mathbb{E}_{\nu_{N,\mu}^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}}})}}\left[\exp\left(\left|\Lambda_{N}\right|s\beta\left(D-D_{K}\right)\right)\right].

We once again rearrange terms to get

g^{\scriptscriptstyle{({K}})}_{N}\left(s\right)=\frac{1}{\beta\left|\Lambda_{N}\right|}\log\mathbb{E}_{\nu_{N,\mu+s}^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}}})}}\left[\exp\left(-\left|\Lambda_{N}\right|s\beta D_{K}\right)\right]+\frac{1}{\beta\left|\Lambda_{N}\right|}\log\frac{Z^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}}})}_{N}\left(\beta,\mu+s\right)}{Z^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}}})}_{N}\left(\beta,\mu\right)}.

Then we want to use Varadhan’s Lemma with our LDP for the PMF measure and the tilt
\phi=-s\beta D_{K}
. This
\phi
is continuous, but we need to pay attention to the boundedness conditions. We will show

\lim_{M\to\infty}\limsup_{N\to\infty}\frac{1}{\left|\Lambda_{N}\right|}\mathbb{E}_{\nu_{N,\mu+s}^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}}})}}\left[{\rm e}^{\left|\Lambda_{N}\right|\phi}\mathds{1}\left\{\phi\geq M\right\}\right]=-\infty.

(4.10)

For
s\geq 0
, we have
\phi\leq 0
almost surely, so (
4.10
) holds trivially. For
s<0
we have to work a little harder. Our LDP for the PMF model gives us a bound on the probability of this set:

\displaystyle\limsup_{N\rightarrow\infty}\frac{1}{\left|\Lambda_{N}\right|}\log\nu^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}}})}_{N,\mu+s}\left(\phi=m\right)

\displaystyle\leq-\inf_{\phi=m}I^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}}})}_{\mu+s}

\displaystyle\frac{m}{\left|s\right|\beta}\geq\frac{\mu+s}{a}\implies\qquad

\displaystyle\leq\inf_{\ell_{1}}\left\{I+\beta H^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}}})}_{\mu+s,l.s.c.}\right\}+\beta(\mu+s)\left(\frac{m}{\lvert s\rvert\beta}\right)-\frac{a\beta}{2}\left(\frac{m}{\lvert s\rvert\beta}\right)^{2}.

This means that given
m\geq\left|s\right|\beta\frac{\mu+s}{a}
, then for sufficiently large
N
there exists a
m
and
N
independent constant
C>\inf_{\ell_{1}}\left\{I_{0}+\beta H^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}}})}_{\mu+s,l.s.c.}\right\}
, such that

{\rm e}^{m\lvert\Lambda_{N}\rvert}\nu^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}}})}_{N,\mu+s}\left(\phi=m\right)\leq\exp\Big(\lvert\Lambda_{N}\rvert\big[C+\frac{\mu}{\lvert s\rvert m}-\frac{a}{2\beta\lvert s\rvert^{2}}m^{2}\big]\Big).

The very fast decay with
m
proves that (
4.10
) holds even for
s<0
, and Varadhan gives us

\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}g^{\scriptscriptstyle{({K}})}_{N}(s)=-\inf_{\ell_{1}}\big\{\frac{1}{\beta}I^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}}})}_{\mu+s}+sD_{J}\big\}+p^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}}})}\left(\beta,\mu+s\right)-p^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}}})}\left(\beta,\mu\right),\qquad\forall s\in\mathbb{R}.

In the style of Lemma
2.4
, we can find that this infimum is achieved at
\xi\left(s\right)\in\ell_{1}\left(\mathbb{R}_{+}\right)
, where

\xi_{k}=q_{k}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({0}})}}\exp\left(\beta k\left[\left(\mu+s-a\delta^{*}\right)_{-}-s\mathds{1}\left\{k\leq J\right\}\right]\right),\quad k\in\mathbb{N},

and
\delta^{*}\left(s\right)
is given implicitly as the unique solution to the equation

\delta^{*}=\begin{cases}\sum^{K}_{j=1}jq_{j}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({0}})}}\exp\left(\beta j\left(\mu-a\delta^{*}\right)\right)+\sum^{\infty}_{j=K+1}jq_{j}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({0}})}}\exp\left(\beta j\left(\mu+s-a\delta^{*}\right)\right)&,\delta^{*}\geq\frac{\mu+s}{a},\\
\sum^{K}_{j=1}jq_{j}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({0}})}}\exp\left(-s\beta j\right)+\sum^{\infty}_{j=K+1}jq_{j}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({0}})}}&,\delta^{*}\leq\frac{\mu+s}{a}.\end{cases}

(4.11)

If we denote

\delta^{*}_{K}=\begin{cases}\sum^{K}_{j=1}jq_{j}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({0}})}}\exp\left(\beta j\left(\mu-a\delta^{*}\right)\right)&,\delta^{*}\geq\frac{\mu+s}{a},\\
\sum^{K}_{j=1}jq_{j}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({0}})}}\exp\left(-s\beta j\right)&,\delta^{*}\leq\frac{\mu+s}{a},\end{cases}

(4.12)

then Lemma
4.1
tells us that

\displaystyle\frac{{\rm d}}{{\rm d}s}\Big(\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}g^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({K}})}}_{N}\left(s\right)\Big)

\displaystyle=\left(\delta^{*}-\delta^{*}_{K}\right)\left(s\right)+\Big(\frac{\mu+s}{a}-\delta^{*}\left(s\right)\Big)_{+}

\displaystyle\frac{{\rm d}}{{\rm d}s}\Big(\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}g^{\scriptscriptstyle{({K}})}_{N}\left(s\right)\Big)\Big|_{s=0}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\left(\delta^{*}-\delta^{*}_{K}\right)\left(0\right)&,\mu\leq a\varrho_{\mathrm{c}},\\
\frac{\mu}{a}-\delta^{*}_{K}\left(0\right)&,\mu\geq a\varrho_{\mathrm{c}},\end{cases}

\displaystyle\lim_{K\to\infty}\frac{{\rm d}}{{\rm d}s}\Big(\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}g^{\scriptscriptstyle{({K}})}_{N}\left(s\right)\Big)\Big|_{s=0}

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{\mu}{a}-\varrho_{\mathrm{c}}\right)_{+}.

∎

The HYL model

Proof of Proposition
2.19
.
This is proven by using Lemma
4.1
with the zero found in Proposition
2.7
. Convexity also follows from Proposition
2.7
, noting that
D\left(\xi^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{792963}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle HYL$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{792963}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle HYL$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{585927}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle HYL$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{585928}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle HYL$}}}}}}})}\right)
is continuous and increasing in
\mu
.
∎

Proof of Proposition
2.20
.
Let us set
\alpha\equiv 0
. Recall that
g^{\chi}\left(\delta\right)
denotes the right hand side of (
2.5
) for a given
\chi
. Now
\beta\geq\beta^{*}
ensures that
g^{\chi}
can be defined for all
\delta\in\mathbb{R}
. For this proof we will only require
\mu\leq\bar{\mu}:=ag^{0}\left(\frac{\mu}{a}\right)=\frac{-1}{b\beta}\sum^{\infty}_{j=1}\frac{1}{j}W_{0}\left(-b\beta j^{2}q^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({0}})}}_{j}\right)
.

Note that for
d\geq 3
, the arguments for the Lambert W functions are strictly increasing in the summation index
k
, approaching
0
. This means that since the difference
W_{0}\left(x\right)-W_{-1}\left(x\right)\geq 0
is strictly increasing in
x
and equals
0
if and only if
x=-{\rm e}^{-1}
, we only need to consider finitely many
\chi
for a given
\mu
(all of which are eventually
0
). Now since any non-convexity in
g^{\chi}
can only arrive via the finitely many
\chi_{k}=-1
terms, solutions to
\delta=g^{\chi}\left(\delta\right)
are locally finite in
\mathbb{R}
. To complement this, note that
\lim_{\delta\to+\infty}g^{0}\left(\delta\right)=0
whilst for
\chi\neq 0
we have
g^{\chi}\left(\delta\right)\gg\delta
. Hence we only need to consider a finite range of
\delta
, and therefore for a given
\mu
there are only finitely many solutions for
\delta
.

Because
g^{\chi}
is continuous for each
\chi
, we can collect solutions uniquely and maximally into continuous paths
\xi^{j}\left(\mu\right)
defined on closed (possibly infinite) intervals
I^{j}
with non-empty interior. We allow families to overlap at endpoints of these intervals. Because we are only considering
\mu\leq\bar{\mu}
and there are only finitely many solutions for each
\mu
, we will only have finitely many families being relevant to our discussion.

For each of these families, we will denote

D^{j}\left(\mu\right):=D\left(\xi^{j}\left(\mu\right)\right),\qquad P^{j}\left(\mu\right):=p\left(\beta,0\right)-\frac{1}{\beta}\left(I+\beta H^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{792963}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle HYL$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{792963}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle HYL$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{585927}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle HYL$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{585928}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle HYL$}}}}}}})}_{\mu,l.s.c.}\right)\left(\xi^{j}\left(\mu\right)\right),

defined on the interval
I^{j}
. From Proposition
2.6
and Theorem
2.8
we know that

p^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{792963}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle HYL$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{792963}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle HYL$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{585927}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle HYL$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{585928}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle HYL$}}}}}}})}\left(\beta,\mu\right)=p\left(\beta,0\right)-\frac{1}{\beta}\inf_{\ell_{1}}\left\{I+\beta H^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{792963}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle HYL$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{792963}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle HYL$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{585927}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle HYL$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{585928}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle HYL$}}}}}}})}_{\mu,l.s.c.}\right\}=\max_{j}P^{j}\left(\mu\right).

Therefore for each
\mu
, there exists a
J
such that
p^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{792963}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle HYL$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{792963}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle HYL$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{585927}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle HYL$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{585928}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle HYL$}}}}}}})}\left(\beta,\mu\right)=P^{J}\left(\mu\right)
.

From the continuity of
g^{\chi}
we know that all
D^{j}
are continuous on their
I^{j}
. Then Lemma
4.1
tells us that each
P^{j}
is differentiable on
\mathrm{Int}I^{j}
, with derivative

\frac{{\rm d}P^{j}}{{\rm d}\mu}=D^{j}+\frac{\left(\mu-aD^{j}\right)_{+}}{a-b}.

Continuity of this derivative follows from the continuity of
D^{j}
.

Let us now consider the
\chi=0
solutions. Since
g^{0}
is convex when restricted to
\delta\leq\frac{\mu}{a}
,
\frac{{\rm d}g^{\chi}}{{\rm d}\delta}\to+\infty
as
\delta\uparrow\frac{\mu}{a}
, and
g^{0}
is decreasing for
\delta\geq\frac{\mu}{a}
, there exists
\underline{\mu}<\bar{\mu}
such that this branch has multiple solutions if and only if
\mu\in\left[\underline{\mu},\bar{\mu}\right]
. Let us label these
\xi^{0}
,
\xi^{1}
, and
\xi^{2}
such that
D^{0}\geq D^{1}\geq D^{2}
. Note that
I^{0}=\left(-\infty,\bar{\mu}\right]
,
I^{1}=\left[\underline{\mu},\bar{\mu}\right]
, and
I^{2}=\left[\underline{\mu},+\infty\right)
. For a visualisation of these solutions, see Figure
3
.

Since
\xi^{0}\left(\bar{\mu}\right)=\xi^{1}\left(\bar{\mu}\right)
and
\xi^{1}\left(\underline{\mu}\right)=\xi^{2}\left(\underline{\mu}\right)
, we have
P^{0}\left(\bar{\mu}\right)=P^{1}\left(\bar{\mu}\right)
and
P^{1}\left(\underline{\mu}\right)=P^{2}\left(\underline{\mu}\right)
. Because
D^{0}\geq\frac{\mu}{a}
and
D^{1,2}\leq\frac{\mu}{a}
, we have

\frac{{\rm d}P^{0}}{{\rm d}\mu}=D^{0},\qquad\frac{{\rm d}P^{1}}{{\rm d}\mu}=\frac{b}{a-b}\left(\frac{\mu}{b}-D^{1}\right)<\frac{b}{a-b}\left(\frac{\mu}{b}-D^{2}\right)=\frac{{\rm d}P^{2}}{{\rm d}\mu},

on
\left(\underline{\mu},\bar{\mu}\right)
. Together these mean that
P^{2}\left(\bar{\mu}\right)>P^{1}\left(\bar{\mu}\right)=P^{0}\left(\bar{\mu}\right)
.

Now extending our attention to all
\xi^{j}
defined on some part of
\left(-\infty,\bar{\mu}\right]
, we define

M:=\left\{\mu\leq\bar{\mu}:\exists j\text{ such that }P^{j}\left(\mu\right)>P^{0}\left(\mu\right)\right\}.

We have just shown that
M\neq\varnothing
, so
\hat{\mu}:=\inf M
is finite. Since
P^{2}
and
P^{0}
are continuous,
\hat{\mu}<\bar{\mu}
.

If
\hat{\mu}\in M
, then
\max_{j}P^{j}\left(\mu\right)
is discontinuous at
\mu=\hat{\mu}
. In this case we are done.

If
\hat{\mu}\notin M
, then since the
P^{j}
are each continuous,
\exists J
and
\epsilon>0
such that
P^{J}\left(\mu\right)>P^{0}\left(\mu\right)
for
\mu\in\left(\hat{\mu},\hat{\mu}+\epsilon\right)
.

Now we have to show that the derivatives of
P^{0}
and
P^{J}
necessarily have different limits as we take
\mu\to\hat{\mu}
from their respective sides. First note that

\lim_{\mu\uparrow\hat{\mu}}\frac{{\rm d}P^{0}}{{\rm d}\mu}=D^{0}\left(\hat{\mu}\right).

Note that
g^{\chi}\left(\delta\right)\geq g^{0}\left(\delta\right)
with equality only if
\chi=0
or if we have both
\beta=\beta^{*}
and
\delta=\frac{\mu}{a}
. Therefore
D^{J}\neq\frac{\mu}{a}
and

\displaystyle D^{J}<\frac{\mu}{a}

\displaystyle\implies D^{J}\in\left[D^{2},D^{1}\right]

\displaystyle D^{J}>\frac{\mu}{a}

\displaystyle\implies D^{J}>D^{0}.

This last inequality is strict because equality could only occur at
\mu=\bar{\mu}
, but
\hat{\mu}<\bar{\mu}
.

If
D^{J}>\frac{\mu}{a}
, then

\lim_{\mu\downarrow\hat{\mu}}\frac{{\rm d}P^{J}}{{\rm d}\mu}=D^{J}\left(\hat{\mu}\right)>D^{0}\left(\hat{\mu}\right)=\lim_{\mu\uparrow\hat{\mu}}\frac{{\rm d}P^{0}}{{\rm d}\mu}

and we are done.

From the symmetry of
g^{\chi}
about
\delta=\frac{\mu}{a}
and from
g^{0}
being decreasing for
\delta\geq\frac{\mu}{a}
, we have

D^{0}-\frac{\mu}{a}<\frac{b}{a-b}\left(\frac{\mu}{a}-D^{1}\right)

(4.13)

for
\mu\in\left[\underline{\mu},\bar{\mu}\right)
. See Figure
6
. This implies that if
D^{J}<\frac{\mu}{a}
,

\frac{{\rm d}P^{J}}{{\rm d}\mu}=\frac{b}{a-b}\left(\frac{\mu}{b}-D^{J}\right)\geq\frac{b}{a-b}\left(\frac{\mu}{b}-D^{1}\right)>D^{0}=\frac{{\rm d}P^{0}}{{\rm d}\mu}.

Taking the limit to
\hat{\mu}
gives our result.

H^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{792963}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle HYL$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{792963}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle HYL$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{585927}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle HYL$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{585928}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle HYL$}}}}}}})}_{\mu,l.s.c.}\geq H^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}}})}_{\mu,a-b,l.s.c.}
almost surely. The proof of (

) uses Lemma

Appendices

Appendix A
Processes for Dirichlet and periodic boundary conditions

For Dirichlet boundary condition, one restricts the Brownian bridges to not leaving the set
\Lambda
. Consider the measure

\mu^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({{\rm Dir},\beta}})}}_{x,y}(A)=\frac{\mathbb{P}_{x}(B\in A;B_{\beta}\in{\rm d}y)}{{\rm d}y},\qquad A\subset{\mathcal{C}}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\rm Dir}})}}_{1,\Lambda}\mbox{ measurable},

(A.1)

which has total mass

g_{\beta}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\rm Dir}})}}(x,y)=\mu_{x,y}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\rm Dir,\beta}})}}({\mathcal{C}}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\rm Dir}})}}_{1,\Lambda})=\frac{\mathbb{P}_{x}(B_{[0,\beta]}\subset\Lambda;B_{\beta}\in{\rm d}y)}{{\rm d}y}.

(A.2)

For periodic boundary condition, the marks are Brownian bridges on the torus
\Lambda=(\mathbb{R}/\penalty L\mathbb{Z})^{d}
. The corresponding path measure is denoted by
\mu_{x,y}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\rm per,\beta}})}}
; its total mass is equal to

g_{\beta}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\rm per}})}}(x,y)=\mu_{x,y}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\rm per,\beta}})}}({\mathcal{C}}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\rm per}})}}_{\Lambda})=\sum_{z\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}g_{\beta}(x,y+zL)=(4\pi\beta)^{-d/2}\sum_{z\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}{\rm e}^{-\frac{|x-y-zL|^{2}}{4\beta}}.

(A.3)

For periodic and Dirichlet boundary conditions (
1.8
) is replaced by

\overline{q}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\rm bc}})}}=\sum_{k=1}^{N}q_{k}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\rm bc}})}},\qquad\mbox{ with }q_{k}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\rm bc}})}}=\frac{1}{k|\Lambda|}\int_{\Lambda}{\rm d}x\,g_{k\beta}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\rm bc}})}}(x,x).

(A.4)

Note that this weight depends on
\Lambda
and on
N
. We introduce the Poisson point process
\omega_{\rm P}=\sum_{x\in\xi_{\rm P}}\delta_{(x,B_{x})}
on
\Lambda\times E^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\rm bc}})}}
with intensity measure
\nu^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\rm bc}})}}
whose projections on
\Lambda\times{\mathcal{C}}_{k,\Lambda}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\rm bc}})}}
with
k\leq N
are equal to
\nu_{k}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\rm bc}})}}({\rm d}x,{\rm d}f)=\frac{1}{k}{\rm Leb}_{\Lambda}({\rm d}x)\otimes\mu_{x,x}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({{\rm bc},k\beta}})}}({\rm d}f)
and are zero on this set for
k>N
. We do not label
\omega_{\rm P}
nor
\xi_{\rm P}
with the boundary condition nor with
N
;
\xi_{\rm P}
is a Poisson process on
\Lambda
with intensity measure
\overline{q}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\rm bc}})}}
times the restriction
{\rm Leb}_{\Lambda}
of the Lebesgue measure to
\Lambda
. By
{\tt Q}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\rm bc}})}}
and
{\tt E}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\rm bc}})}}
we denote probability and expectation with respect to this process. Conditionally on
\xi_{\rm P}
, the lengths of the cycles
B_{x}
with
x\in\xi_{\rm P}
are independent and have distribution
(q_{k}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\rm bc}})}}/\overline{q}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\rm bc}})}})_{k\in\{1,\dots,N\}}
; this process has only marks with lengths
\leq N
. A cycle
B_{x}
of length
k
is distributed according to

\mathbb{P}_{x,x}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({{\rm bc},k\beta}})}}({\rm d}f)=\frac{\mu_{x,x}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({{\rm bc},k\beta}})}}({\rm d}f)}{g^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\rm bc}})}}_{k\beta}(x,x)}.

(A.5)

The above representations allows us to prove the large deviation principles for different boundary conditions. For details we refer to
[
ACK11
]
where these arguments are presented in detail for higher level large deviation principles. The independence of the thermodynamic limit of the average finite-volume pressure in Theorem
2.8
follows using either the arguments in
[
ACK11
]
or in
[
Rob71
,
AN73
,
BR97
]
.

Appendix B
Bose function

The Bose functions are poly-logarithmic functions defined by

g(n,\alpha):=\operatorname{Li}\,_{n}({\rm e}^{-\alpha})=\frac{1}{\Gamma(n)}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{t^{n-1}}{{\rm e}^{t+\alpha}-1}\,{\rm d}t=\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}k^{-n}{\rm e}^{-\alpha k}\quad\mbox{ for all }n\mbox{ and }\alpha>0,

(B.1)

and also for
\alpha=0
and
n>1
. In the latter case,

g(n,0)=\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}k^{-n}=\zeta(n),

(B.2)

which is the zeta function of Riemann. The behaviour of the Bose functions about
\alpha=0
is given by

g(n,\alpha)=\begin{cases}\Gamma(1-n)\alpha^{n-1}+\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\zeta(n-k)\frac{(-\alpha)^{k}}{k!}&,n\not=1,2,3,\ldots,\\[6.45831pt]
\frac{(-\alpha)^{n-1}}{(n-1)!}\Big[-\log\alpha+\sum_{m=1}^{n-1}\frac{1}{m}\Big]+\sum_{\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{k=0}{k\not= n-1}}\zeta(n-k)\frac{(-\alpha)^{k}}{k!}&,n\in\mathbb{N}.\end{cases}

(B.3)

At
\alpha=0
,
g(n,\alpha)
diverges for
n\leq 1
, indeed for all
n
there is some kind of singularity at
\alpha=0
, such as a branch point. For further details see
[
Gra25
]
. The expansions (
B.3
) are in terms of
\zeta(n)
, which for for
n\leq 1
must be found by analytic ally continuing (
B.2
). With the asymptotic properties of the zeta function it can be shown that the
k
series in (
B.3
) are convergent for
|\alpha|<2\pi
. Consequently (
B.3
) also represents an analytic continuation of
g(n,\alpha)
for
\alpha<0
. When
\alpha\gg 1
the series (
B.1
) itself is rapidly convergent, and as
\alpha\to\infty
,
g(n,\alpha)\sim{\rm e}^{-\alpha}
for all
n
. Some plots for the Bose functions are given in Figure
7
.

Figure 7
.

Appendix C
Lambert W function

The Lambert W function (sometimes called elsewhere the
Omega function
) is defined as the multi-valued inverse of the
\mathbb{C}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
function
w\mapsto w{\rm e}^{w}
. We shall only be concerned with the two branches on
\mathbb{R}
. Figure
8
shows these two real branches, denoted
W_{0}
and
W_{-1}
. The
W_{0}
branch is defined on
\left[-{\rm e}^{-1},\infty\right)
, whereas the
W_{-1}
branch is only defined on
\left[-{\rm e}^{-1},0\right)
. Given a branch
W_{l}
with
l\in\left\{0,-1\right\}
, we can find its (real) derivative
W^{\prime}_{l}
by differentiating the equation
W_{l}\left(x\right){\rm e}^{W_{l}\left(x\right)}=x
. This gives us

W^{\prime}_{l}\left(x\right)=\frac{1}{x}\frac{W_{l}\left(x\right)}{1+W_{l}\left(x\right)}.

Taking further derivatives and applying induction shows that the branches are smooth on the interior of their respective domains, and gives expressions for each order of the derivative. We make use of some asymptotic expansions of
W_{0}
and
W_{1}
:

\displaystyle W_{0}\left(x\right)

\displaystyle=x-x^{2}+o\left(x^{2}\right)

\displaystyle\text{as }x\to 0,

\displaystyle W_{0}\left(x\right)

\displaystyle=\log x-\log\left(\log x\right)+o\left(1\right)

\displaystyle\text{as }x\to+\infty,

\displaystyle W_{-1}\left(x\right)

\displaystyle=\log\left(-x\right)-\log\left(-\log\left(-x\right)\right)+o\left(1\right)

\displaystyle\text{as }x\uparrow 0.

For more details, see
[
CGHJK96
]
.

x
W\left(x\right)

W_{-1}

W
:

and
